     16-bit SAR ADC Oscilloscope + 3-Mode Operation — Integration Plan

     SAR Algorithm Reference (for understanding the firmware)

     See explanation in conversation. Key facts for this circuit:
     - N=16 bits → always exactly 16 comparisons per sample
     - Each step: set 1 bit HIGH, output to DAC, wait 10µs, read PD2
     - PD2 HIGH (6N137 off) = DAC > signal → CLEAR the bit
     - PD2 LOW  (6N137 on)  = signal > DAC → KEEP the bit
     - 16 × 10µs = 160µs per conversion → ~6kSPS in OSC mode
     - Result 0x8000 = 0V (probe 0V + 2.5V bias = 2.5V at comparator)

     ---
     Context

     User wants a 16-bit DIY SAR oscilloscope added to the ATmega32A function
     generator,
     with three operating modes. No HCPL-7840 available. One 6N137 digital
     optocoupler on hand.
     Extra CD4051 available for future multi-channel input expansion.

     Why lower frequency in BOTH mode:
     FG uses PORTC (8-bit, 40kHz direct write) and OSC uses SPI 595s (16-bit).
     These are
     physically separate R-2R ladders on different pins — they CAN run
     simultaneously.
     However, the SAR conversion takes ~160µs (16 steps × 10µs). During those
     160µs the CPU
     can't also compute DDS + SPI. Timer2 ISR still fires and updates PORTC, but
     the main
     loop SAR monopolises SPI during capture. This creates timing jitter on SAR
     settle steps.
     Clean solution: in BOTH mode, halve the DDS sample rate (20kHz → 10kHz max
     FG output).
     CPU gets 50µs windows between ISR calls, enough to advance one SAR step per
     window.

     ---
     Three Modes

     ┌────────┬─────────┬──────────┬─────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
     │  Mode  │ Command │ FG max   │ OSC sample  │             How             │
     │        │         │   freq   │    rate     │                             │
     ├────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ FG     │ m0      │ 20kHz    │ off         │ Timer2 @ 40kHz, PORTC, no   │
     │ only   │         │          │             │ SAR                         │
     ├────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ OSC    │ m1      │ off      │ ~6kSPS      │ Timer2 off, SAR runs in     │
     │ only   │         │          │             │ main loop                   │
     ├────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ BOTH   │ m2      │ 10kHz    │ ~1kSPS      │ Timer2 @ 20kHz, SAR         │
     │        │         │          │             │ interleaved with ISR        │
     └────────┴─────────┴──────────┴─────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

     ---
     Hardware Architecture (oscilloscope subsystem — separate from function gen)

     Probe
       │
     [Fuse 100mA]──[MOV S14K275]──[R_in 10kΩ 1W]
       │
     [1N4148 to GND] ← negative spike clamp → keeps input ≥ −0.7V
     [1N4148 to +5V] ← positive spike clamp → keeps input ≤ 5.7V
       │
     [R_bias 10kΩ to 2.5V ref]   ← shifts 0V → 2.5V for negative voltage
     measurement
       │
     NE5532-B (+) pin 5  ────────────── signal under test

     16-bit R-2R DAC (2× 74HC595) → NE5532-B (−) pin 6  (SAR reference)

     NE5532-B output (open-loop ±13V)
       │
     [820Ω]──[1N4148]──── 6N137 LED anode
                          6N137 LED cathode ── GND
                          6N137 pin 8 (VCC) ── +5V
                          6N137 pin 7 (VE)  ── GND
                          6N137 pin 6 (Vo)  ──[4.7kΩ]── +5V ── ATmega PD2

     Negative voltage (PD2 protected by 6N137 — no current backflow even if probe
     fails):
     - 2.5V bias → probe 0V = comparator input 2.5V = SAR result 0x8000
     - probe −2.5V = comparator 0.5V = result 0x0000 (min)
     - probe +2.5V = comparator 4.5V = result 0xFFFF (max)
     - For ±10V range: add ÷4 divider before bias node, set v_fullscale=10.0 in
     firmware

     Safety: Not safe for mains voltages (no isolation). Bench/low-voltage use
     only (≤30V).

     ---
     16-bit R-2R SAR DAC (new 595 chain, separate from existing function gen 595)

     PB5 (MOSI) ── IC1-DS ── IC1-Q7' ── IC2-DS  (high byte first, MSB first)
     PB7 (SCK)  ── IC1/IC2 SRCLK
     PB3 (new)  ── IC1/IC2 RCLK latch  (separate from PB4 used by existing chain)
     IC1-Q7 → VOUT (0–5V) ── NE5532-B (−)
     IC2-Q0 ──[20kΩ]── GND (2R termination)

     Resolution: 5V / 65536 = 76µV/step. With 1% resistors, effective accuracy
     ~8–10 bits
     (256–1024 usable levels = ~5mV–20mV resolution over 5V range). Adequate for
     oscilloscope.

     ---
     ATmega32A Pin Allocation



     ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │     Pin     │                  Function                   │
     ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ PC0–PC7     │ DDS R-2R (function gen, 8-bit, Timer2 ISR)  │
     ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ PB5         │ SPI MOSI (shared)                           │
     ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
     │ PB7         │ SPI SCK (shared)                            │
     ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤

     ├─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤

     └─────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

     NE5532-A: available if HCPL-7840 isolation is added later.

     ---
     New Firmware Files

     src/drivers/dac16.h

     void     dac16_init(void);          // DDRB |= PB3; SPI init (shares 
     existing SPI)
     void     dac16_write(uint16_t val); // shift high byte then low byte, pulse 
     PB3 latch

     src/drivers/sar_osc.h

     uint16_t sar_convert(void);                         // 16-bit binary search 
     (~160µs)
     float    sar_to_volts(uint16_t code, float v_full); // code 0x8000 = 0V
     void     sar_stream_n(uint16_t n_samples);          // stream n samples over
     UART

     SAR logic (6N137 output is inverted: LED on → pin 6 LOW):
     uint16_t sar_convert(void) {
         uint16_t result = 0;
         for (int8_t bit = 15; bit >= 0; bit--) {
             result |= (1 << bit);
             dac16_write(result);
             _delay_us(10);
             if (PIND & (1 << PD2))         // 6N137 HIGH = DAC > signal → clear 
     bit
                 result &= ~(1 << bit);
         }
         return result;
     }

     src/drivers/mode.h

     typedef enum { MODE_FG=0, MODE_OSC=1, MODE_BOTH=2 } op_mode_t;
     void mode_set(op_mode_t m);
     // MODE_FG:   restores Timer2 @ 40kHz, disables SAR
     // MODE_OSC:  stops Timer2 (TCCR2=0), enables SAR stream
     // MODE_BOTH: sets Timer2 @ 20kHz (OCR2=99), SAR interleaved

     UART commands added to src/drivers/cmd.h

❯ basicalyl i am trying to fidn best operation resistor networks and circuit    

● I have the full circuit in context (netlist + the math files I wrote), so no
  codebase exploration is needed — this is a pure analog-design optimization on
  a circuit I already understand. Let me formulate it, capture it in the plan
  file, then confirm the few free parameters with you.

  The unifying insight

  All three switched-resistor banks are the same structure — a 4-bit DAC made of
  switched conductances into an op-amp virtual ground:

  - U17 (signal amplitude) — 4 resistors from U7's output → U20 virtual ground
  - U21 (offset amplitude) — 4 resistors from U9's output → U22 virtual ground
  - U23 (centering, internal/−8V) — 4 resistors from −8V → U16 virtual ground

  When several CD4066 switches close, their resistors sit in parallel, so
  conductances add:

  $$Y(\mathbf{s})=\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{s_i}{R_i},\qquad s_i\in{0,1}$$

  The bank's effect (gain, or injected voltage) is linear in $Y$. Your phrase
  "use one resistor and scale it" is exactly the optimal answer: pick
  binary-weighted conductances $g_i = 2^i g_0$ (i.e. $R,;R/2,;R/4,;R/8$). Then
  the 4-bit code $n\in{0,\dots,15}$ gives $Y = n,g_0$ — 16 uniform levels from 
  one base resistance. That is provably the maximum number of evenly-spaced
  levels from 4 switches, and it's monotonic.

  This reduces the whole "optimization" to: choose one base resistor per bank to
  set full-scale, take binary multiples, round to E-series, and let firmware 
  calibration absorb the rounding (you already accepted a calibration LUT for
  the 5% parts).

  Let me record this formulation in the plan file before I lock the numbers.

● Great instinct — and yes, "more than 6" is right. Let me set those resistors
  as unknowns, answer your count question, and teach the structure, because
  there's a key concept here that makes the whole thing click.

  First — the Sallen-Key resistors: NO, don't make them variable ✓

  You're correct. R6, R18, R8, R19 (10k) on U6A/U6B set the filter cutoff 
  frequency, not amplitude or offset:

  $$f_c \approx \frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{R_6 R_{18},C_{16}C_{19}}}$$

  They belong to a separate subsystem (anti-alias / smoothing). They're fixed by
  the bandwidth you want (~16 kHz with 10k+1nF), and they sit after the summing
  amp where the signal level is already final. Leave them fixed. Adding them to
  the amplitude/offset system would just couple your filter to your gain — bad.

  The new unknowns you asked for

  Now treating these as variables too:
  - R15 (U16 feedback), R17 (offset in), R20 (signal in) — the summing weights
  - R25 (U20 feedback) and R30 (U22 feedback) — the two post-amp gains

  How many equations so far?

  In output_stage_math.qmd we already wrote 8 numbered equations + 4 named 
  constraints (A–D). The independent design constraints among them are about 6:

  ┌─────┬────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │  #  │                Equation                │       What it pins        │
  ├─────┼────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 1   │ Centering: maps DAC → symmetric signal │ $R_\text{cen}=32\text{k}$ │
  ├─────┼────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 2   │ Peak output = rail at max gain         │ links $G_\max$, $R_7$     │
  ├─────┼────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 3   │ $G_\max = R_{25}/R_{g,\min}$           │ gain bank top             │
  ├─────┼────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 4   │ DAC half-swing $=2.5\text{V}$          │ given                     │
  ├─────┼────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 5   │ Centered mode: $D = 2.5G$              │ centering value           │
  ├─────┼────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 6   │ Rail limit: $                          │ D                         │
  └─────┴────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  With your new unknowns we add ~4 more structural equations:

  ┌─────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  #  │                    Equation                     │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 7   │ Signal weight: $w_s = R_{15}/R_{20}$            │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8   │ Offset weight: $w_o = R_{15}/R_{17}$            │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 9   │ Offset gain: $H_o = R_{30}\cdot\sum s_i/R_{oi}$ │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10  │ Centering DAC: $V_c = 8,R_{15}\sum s_i/R_{ci}$  │
  └─────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So: ~10 equations now. Your "more than 6" was spot on.

  The concept that makes this solvable — degrees of freedom

  Here's the thing to learn. Count it:

  - Unknown resistors: ~15 (R7, R13, R20, R17, R15, R25, R30, + 3 banks × ~base
  each, + R_cen)
  - Equations: ~10

  More unknowns than equations → the system is under-determined. There is no
  single solution — there's a family of them. That's not a bug; it's the design 
  freedom. The optimization is choosing the free variables wisely.

  Why so much freedom? Because an inverting amp's gain depends only on the 
  resistor ratio, not the absolute values:

  $$\text{gain} = -\frac{R_\text{fb}}{R_\text{in}}$$

  Each stage gives you 1 equation (the gain) but 2 resistors → 1 free choice per
  stage. You spend that freedom on a secondary constraint:

  - Too small (<1k): draws too much current, loads the previous stage
  - Too big (>1M): thermal noise + TL071 bias-current offset grow
  - Sweet spot: 10k–100k → this is why feedbacks default to 10k

  So the ratios come from the gain math; the absolute scale comes from "keep it
  in the 10k–100k sweet spot." That single rule pins all the leftover degrees of
  freedom.

  This is also why the summing weights (R15/R17/R20) being equal = unity is the
  clean choice: it puts all scaling in the dedicated gain banks and leaves U16
  as a pure adder. Making them unequal is redundant freedom you don't need.

  ---
  Before I formalize the full system in the plan, let me confirm the two
  decisions that collapse most of the free variables — these set how we spend
  the degrees of freedom:

     · Should the U16 summing weights (R15/R17/R20) be equal (unity adder),

● You've given me enough to solve the whole thing. Two important teaching points
  before I write it up, because your new info changes the optimal answer.

  Key insight: 8-bit analog amplitude is redundant — and would be non-monotonic

  You said amplitude + offset will be 8-bit (second CD4066 each). Two problems
  with 8-bit binary-weighted switched resistors:

  1. Monotonicity: a binary-weighted current DAC needs the MSB resistor matched
  to better than ½ LSB = 1/512 = 0.2%. With 1% (let alone 5%) resistors, the
  code 0x80 step is smaller than 0x7F → the output goes down when the code goes
  up. Calibration cannot fix non-monotonicity — a LUT can only remap a monotonic
  curve. So 8-bit binary switched-R is fundamentally broken with real
  resistors.
  2. Redundancy: you already have a 16-bit DAC feeding each path. Fine amplitude
  is free in software — multiply the wavetable samples by an amplitude factor
  before sending to the DAC. That's 16-bit-clean, perfectly monotonic, zero
  extra parts.

  So the optimal split is: analog bank = a few coarse gain ranges (1 CD4066, 4
  binary bits → ×1/×2/×4/×8), and fine amplitude/offset = digital in the 16-bit
  DACs. This beats 8-bit analog on resolution and monotonicity, and saves the
  second CD4066.

  Centering: you were right — it collapses to ONE resistor

  Solving the centering equation (pre-gain, at U7):

  $$R_\text{cen} = \frac{-V_\text{ref},R_\text{dac}}{V_m} = \frac{8\cdot
  10\text{k}}{2.5} = 32,\text{k}\Omega$$

  A single 32k resistor from −8V into U7 centers the signal before the gain
  stage, so it's automatically centered at every gain — no switching, no bank,
  gain-independent. The whole U23 CD4066 centering bank disappears. Drift/aging
  → trimmed in software via the offset DAC, exactly as you guessed.

  Summing weights — yes, equations give them (you asked)

  The constraint "each path reaches the same ±15V full-scale at the output"
  gives:

  $$\frac{R_{15}}{R_{20}} = \frac{V_\text{out,fs}}{V_\text{SIGNAL,fs}} =
  \frac{15}{15} = 1 ;\Rightarrow; R_{15}=R_{17}=R_{20}$$

  So unity isn't an assumption — it's forced by equal full-scale. Pick 10k
  (sweet spot).

  Let me read the existing plan file and replace it with the complete solved
  system.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Here's the complete solved system. I've put the full derivation in the plan;
  summary for you to follow:

  The full equation system (~11 relations)

  (E1)  Centering at U7:   −(R7/R_dac)·2.5 + (R7/R_cen)·8 = 0  →  R_cen = 32k
  [R7 cancels]
  (E2)  Stage-1 scale:     R7 = R_dac = 10k                    →  V1 = ±2.5V
  (unity)
  (E3)  Max amplitude:     G_max = 15 / 2.5 = 6
  (E4)  Gain bank:         G = R25 · Σ(s_i/R_gi)               [binary → uniform
  ranges]
  (E5)  Pick R25 = 60k:    G_max = 60k/10k = 6 ✓
  (E6)  Signal full-scale: V_SIGNAL,fs = 6·2.5 = 15V
  (E7)  Summing weight:    R15/R20 = 15/15 = 1     →  R15 = R20
  (E8)  Offset path = mirror of signal (R13=10k, R_cen2=32k, R30=60k, R15/R17=1)
  (E9)  Summing scale:     R15 = R17 = R20 = 10k   [sweet spot]
  (E10) Rail limit:        |offset| + amplitude ≤ 15V   [firmware]
  (E11) Sallen-Key:        f_c = 1/(2π√(R6·R18·C16·C19))  [separate, fixed]

  Solved values

  ┌───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │         Resistor          │        Value        │         From          │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ R7, R13 (stage-1 fb)      │ 10k                 │ E2                    │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ R_cen1, R_cen2 (−8V       │ 32k                 │ E1 — single resistor, │
  │ centering)                │                     │  no bank              │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ R25, R30 (post-amp fb)    │ 60k                 │ E5                    │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Gain & offset banks       │ 150k, 75k, 37.5k,   │ E4                    │
  │ (4-bit binary)            │ 18.75k              │                       │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ R15, R17, R20 (summing)   │ 10k                 │ E7/E9                 │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Sallen-Key                │ 10k (unchanged)     │ E11                   │
  └───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  Degrees of freedom: ~15 unknowns − ~11 equations ≈ 4 free → all pinned by the
  10k–100k sweet-spot rule. That's the lesson: ratios from the gain math, 
  absolutes from the noise/bias compromise.

  Two things I changed from your stated plan (flagged for you)

  1. 8-bit analog → 4-bit coarse + 16-bit digital fine. Binary 8-bit switched-R
  is non-monotonic with real resistors (calibration can't fix that), and your
  16-bit DACs already give fine control in software for free. Saves the 2nd
  CD4066 per bank.
  2. U23 centering bank → single 32k resistor. The centering equation collapses
  to one resistor at U7; drift trimmed in software via the offset DAC.

  Both are documented in the plan with reasoning, so you can overrule if you
  want the literal 8-bit analog (I'd then switch it to R-2R topology, not
  binary).

     Function Generator — Output-Stage Resistor Network: Full Solved System

     Context

     The analog output chain (signal path, offset path, centering, final summing
     amp) was
     populated with decade-spaced resistors copied from an old DAC0808 design.
     They attenuate
     instead of amplify, leave gaps, and can't reach ±15V. Goal: derive every
     resistor from a
     proper equation system, keep BOTH 16-bit R-2R DACs, and produce a
     professional-style
     output: bipolar, centered-by-default (firmware), ±15V max amplitude,
     bipolar offset.

     User decisions captured:
     - Resistor absolute scale: 10k–100k sweet spot.
     - Summing weights: derive from equations (not assumed).
     - Amplitude + offset fine control: user proposed 8-bit analog (2nd CD4066).
     Replaced by
     4-bit coarse analog range + 16-bit digital fine (see Decision note) —
     better resolution,
     monotonic, fewer parts. Flag for user confirmation.
     - Centering: collapses to a single resistor (no CD4066 bank needed);
     software trims drift.

     ---
     Variables and givens

     Given:  R_dac = 10k (R-2R Thévenin),  Vm = 2.5 V,  Vs ∈ [0,5],  V_ref = −8
     V (internal),
             V_rail = ±15 V ideal (±13.5 V usable on TL071).
     Unknowns: R7, R13 (stage-1 fb); R_cen1, R_cen2 (centering); R25, R30
     (post-amp fb);
               gain/offset range banks; R15, R17, R20 (summing). Sallen-Key
     R6/R8/R18/R19 fixed.

     ---
     The equation system (≈11 relations)

     (E1)  Centering (pre-gain, at U7):  −(R7/R_dac)·Vm + (R7/R_cen1)·8 = 0
             ⇒  R_cen1 = 8·R_dac/Vm = 8·10k/2.5 = 32 kΩ      (R7 cancels)
     (E2)  Stage-1 scale (sweet spot, unity):  R7 = R_dac = 10 kΩ   ⇒ V1 =
     −(Vs−2.5), ±2.5 V
     (E3)  Max amplitude = rail:  G_max = V_rail/(Vm) = 15/2.5 = 6
     (E4)  Gain bank:  G = R25 · Σ s_i/R_gi   (binary conductances → uniform
     ranges)
     (E5)  Pick R25 = 60 kΩ ⇒ G = (60k/R_g) ; smallest R_g = 10k ⇒ G_max = 6 ✓
     (E6)  Signal full-scale: V_SIGNAL,fs = G·Vm = 6·2.5 = 15 V
     (E7)  Summing weight (equal full-scale): R15/R20 = V_out,fs/V_SIGNAL,fs = 1
     ⇒ R15=R20
     (E8)  Offset path mirrors signal: R13=10k, R_cen2=32k, R30=60k, R15/R17=1 ⇒
     R15=R17
     (E9)  Summing scale (sweet spot): R15=R17=R20 = 10 kΩ
     (E10) Rail constraint (firmware): |offset| + amplitude_peak ≤ V_rail
     (E11) Sallen-Key (separate): f_c = 1/(2π√(R6 R18 C16 C19)) — fixed, not in
     this system

     Degrees of freedom: ~15 unknowns − ~11 relations ≈ 4 free, all absorbed by
     the
     "10k–100k sweet spot" rule (E2, E5, E9) — that's why ratios come from math,
     absolutes
     from the noise/bias compromise.

     ---
     Solved resistor set

     ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬────────┐
     │       Ref        │       Role       │          Value           │ Source │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │                  │ R-2R Thévenin    │                          │        │
     │ R_dac            │ (both            │ 10k (given)              │ —      │
     │                  │ signal+offset    │                          │        │
     │                  │ DACs)            │                          │        │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │ R_cen1           │ centering into   │ 32k (E96 32.4k)          │ E1     │
     │                  │ U7 (from −8V)    │                          │        │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │ R_cen2           │ centering into   │ 32k                      │ E1/E8  │
     │                  │ U9 (from −8V)    │                          │        │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │ R7, R13          │ U7, U9 feedback  │ 10k                      │ E2     │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │ R25, R30         │ U20, U22         │ 60k                      │ E5     │
     │                  │ feedback         │                          │        │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │ Gain bank (U17,  │                  │ 150k, 75k, 37.5k, 18.75k │        │
     │ 1× CD4066, 4-bit │ coarse range     │  (E96:                   │ E4     │
     │  binary)         │                  │ 150k/75k/37.4k/18.7k)    │        │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │ Offset bank      │ coarse range     │ 150k, 75k, 37.5k, 18.75k │ E4/E8  │
     │ (U21, same)      │                  │                          │        │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │ R15, R17, R20    │ U16 summing      │ 10k each                 │ E7/E9  │
     ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼────────┤
     │ Sallen-Key       │ filter           │ 10k (unchanged)          │ E11    │
     │ R6/R8/R18/R19    │                  │                          │        │
     └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴────────┘

     Fine amplitude & fine offset = digital: scale the 16-bit DAC samples in
     firmware.
     Analog bank only sets the coarse ×1/×2/×4/×8 range. Net resolution ≈
     16-bit, monotonic.

     Eliminated: U23 centering CD4066 bank + R31–R34 → replaced by the single
     32k (R_cen1).
     Eliminated: the proposed 2nd CD4066 per bank (8-bit analog) → digital fine
     instead.

     ---
     Decision note — why NOT 8-bit analog amplitude/offset

     1. Binary-weighted 8-bit switched-R needs MSB matched to <0.2% to stay
     monotonic; with
     1–5% resistors code 0x80 < 0x7F → non-monotonic. A calibration LUT cannot 
     fix
     non-monotonicity (it can only remap a monotonic curve). So 8-bit binary =
     broken.
     2. The 16-bit DACs already give monotonic, 16-bit fine control in software
     for free.
     Coarse analog range (4-bit) + digital fine ⇒ better than 8-bit analog,
     fewer parts.
     (If analog 8-bit is truly required, it must be R-2R topology, not
     binary-weighted.)

     ---
     Files to update (on approval)

     1. output_stage_math.qmd — replace with the full derivation above: variable
     table,
     the 11 numbered equations with LaTeX, the solved set, the 8-bit decision
     note, and the
     amplitude/offset/centering/mode equations already present.
     2. CIRCUIT_CHANGES.md — finalize resistor values; mark U23 bank +
     2nd-CD4066 as removed;
     note digital fine-control firmware requirement.
     3. KiCad function_generator.kicad_sch (manual by user, or via kicad MCP
     later): change
     R7/R13→10k, R25/R30→60k, gain/offset banks→150k/75k/37.5k/18.75k,
     R15/R17/R20→10k,
     add R_cen1/R_cen2 = 32k from −8V to U7/U9 inverting nodes, remove U23 +
     R31–R34.

     Verification (bench, after build)

     - Set each gain-bank code; measure V_SIGNAL; confirm uniform ×1/×2/×4/×8
     ranges.
     - Default (offset DAC mid, gain set): confirm output symmetric about 0 V at
     every range.
     - Sweep offset DAC; confirm bipolar offset; confirm |offset|+amp clips at
     ~±13.5 V not before.
     - Build firmware calibration LUT mapping (gain code, DAC code) → measured
     Vout.

      219 -$$                                                            
      220 -\begin{aligned}                                               
      221 -R_\text{cen} &= 32\ \text{k}\Omega &&\text{(centering, from }-
          -8\text{ V into U7)}\\                                         
      222 -R_7 = R_{13} &= 10\ \text{k}\Omega &&\text{(U7, U9 feedback — 
          -unity, derived)}\\                                            
      223 -R_{f2} &= 60\ \text{k}\Omega &&\text{(U20 feedback)}\\        
      224 -R_g &\in \{60,30,20,15,12,10\}\ \text{k}\Omega &&\text{(gain b
          -ank, } \times1\ldots\times6)\\                                
      225 -R_{15}=R_{17}=R_{20} &= 4.7\ \text{k}\Omega &&\text{(U16 summi
          -ng, unchanged)}

      227 -$$                                                            
      225 +1. A binary-weighted 8-bit switched-resistor DAC needs the MSB
          + matched to                                                   
      226 +   $<\tfrac12\,\text{LSB}=1/512\approx0.2\%$ to stay **monoton
          +ic**. With 1 % (or 5 %)                                       
      227 +   resistors the $\mathtt{0x80}$ step is smaller than $\mathtt
          +{0x7F}$ → the output goes *down*                              
      228 +   as the code goes *up*. **A calibration LUT cannot fix non-m
          +onotonicity** — it can only                                   
      229 +   remap a monotonic curve.                                   
      230 +2. The two 16-bit DACs already provide monotonic, 16-bit fine 
          +control in firmware for free.                                 
      231 +   **Coarse analog range (4-bit) + digital fine** therefore be
          +ats 8-bit analog on resolution                                
      232 +   *and* monotonicity, with fewer parts.                      
      233 +3. If an all-analog 8-bit control is ever truly required, it m
          +ust use **R-2R topology**                                     
      234 +   (two resistor values, inherently monotonic), not a binary-w
          +eighted switched bank.                                        
      235  
      229 -Both 16-bit DACs are retained: the signal DAC + $R_\text{cen}=

● Good push. I dug deeper and I did find things we missed — one of them is
  serious. And no, we hadn't written the feature/requirement list anywhere. Let
  me answer the centering question, then show you the gaps, then write the
  requirements list as a doc.

  Centering CD4066 (U23): drop it — confirmed

  The single 32k resistor handles centering at every amplitude, including
  digital scaling, because:
  - Digital amplitude scaling stays symmetric around code 0x8000 (2.5V) → the
  center never moves off 2.5V
  - The 32k subtracts exactly 2.5V before gain → output stays centered on 0 at
  all gains

  The only thing that can drift the center is error sources — 32k resistor
  tolerance, −8V rail drift, and TL071 input offset (Vos). But all three produce
  a DC error that the offset DAC cancels in software (a per-gain-range
  calibration value). A switched centering bank can't do anything the offset DAC
  + calibration can't. So U23 + R31–R34 go. One caveat: the calibration table
  is 2-D — (gain range) → offset correction — because the centering error is
  amplified differently at each gain.

  Deeper review — what we missed ⚠️

  I checked the netlist rails and the signal path. Findings, worst first:

  1. 🔴 The CD4051 output mux is powered ±8V — it CANNOT pass a ±15V signal.
  The chain is: gain stage → U16 → MUX_A0 → CD4051 (U18, ±8V rails) → buffer →
  BNC. Your variable gain happens before the mux, so the full-amplitude signal
  hits a ±8V part and clips at ±8V. This directly breaks the ±15V goal. CD4051
  max is ±10V, so you can't just repower it. Fix options:
  - (a) Move the gain + offset stage to after the mux (the proper architecture:
  DAC → filter → mux at low level ±2.5V → then amplify to ±15V at the output
  stage). This is the clean fix.
  - (b) Accept ±8V max output (no rework).
  - (c) Replace CD4051 with a ±15V analog switch (e.g. DG419) or relay.

  2. 🟠 TL071 on ±15V only swings ~±13.5V. Designed-for ±15V output clips ~1.5V
  early. Either firmware-limit amplitude to ±13.5V, or run the final op-amp on
  ±18V.

  3. 🟠 Output can't drive 50Ω at high voltage. TL071 sources ~±20mA; 15V into
  50Ω = 300mA. High-impedance loads (scope, 1MΩ) only — fine for bench, but
  document it.

  4. 🟡 Low-amplitude digital scaling loses resolution. At 1% amplitude you only
  use ~650 DAC codes (~9 bits). Firmware must drop the analog gain range and
  raise digital scale to keep codes high — that's the whole point of having
  both, but it must be coordinated in firmware.

  5. 🟡 Oscilloscope DAC sharing. The SAR scope needs a DAC as its comparator
  reference. In OSC-only mode the signal DAC is free → reuse it. In BOTH mode
  the signal DAC is busy doing DDS → the scope must fall back to the internal 
  10-bit ADC. Needs to be explicit.

  6. 🟢 Slew rate. Square-wave edges ~2.3µs at full ±15V (13V/µs). Fine below
  ~100kHz.

  Let me write the full feature/requirement list with verification into a doc.

● No — I won't touch the KiCad file. You're right to be cautious. Reasons:

  1. The design isn't final yet. GAP-1 (the CD4051 ±8V limit capping output at
  ±8V) needs an architecture decision first — possibly moving the gain stage
  after the mux. Changing resistor values before that is premature and could
  lock in a layout that has to be undone.
  2. Editing a live schematic through the MCP is risky. It can desync the
  schematic/PCB, disturb wires or annotations, and a wrong write is hard to
  cleanly reverse. Not worth it for a value change you can do safely in seconds.
  3. You should make value edits yourself in KiCad, where you can see each
  component and verify — that's safer than me poking at it blind.

  So I'm leaving the .kicad_sch untouched. The deliverables are the docs, which
  have the exact values and reasons:

  - output_stage_math.qmd — full derivation + solved values
  - CIRCUIT_CHANGES.md — per-resistor change list
  - REQUIREMENTS.md — features, verification, and the gaps (incl. the CD4051
  issue)

  When you edit in KiCad, the value changes are just: R7/R13→10k, R25/R30→60k,
  gain/offset banks→150k/75k/37.5k/18.75k, R15/R17/R20→10k, add 32k centering,
  remove U23 bank — but hold off until you decide GAP-1, because that determines
  where the gain stage (and those resistors) actually live.

  Want to settle GAP-1 first?

● Great — your reorder instinct is exactly the right fix. Let me work through
  all six with the new architecture.

  Gap 1 — Reorder: filter + mux BEFORE gain, and share the ladder with the scope
  ✓

  New signal chain (the ±15V now only exists after the mux, so the CD4051 never
  sees more than ±2.5V):

  Signal R-2R DAC (0–5V, always full 16-bit)
     │
     ├──────────────────────────────► SAR comparator reference (oscilloscope)
     │
   U7  center + unity  → ±2.5V (centered on 0)
     │
   Sallen-Key LPF(s)   → ±2.5V        ← NE5532, small signal, well within ±8V
     │
   CD4051 mux (smooth / raw select)   ← now safe: only ±2.5V passes ✓
     │
   GAIN stage (×1…×5)  +  OFFSET sum  ← the ±15V is created HERE, after the mux
     │
   Output buffer + 50Ω → BNC

  Two wins: the CD4051 limit disappears, and we can merge the old U20 (gain) +
  U16 (summing) into one final stage (gain on the signal input, offset on a
  second input). Fewer parts.

  Ladder shared with scope: the raw 0–5V ladder output taps off to the SAR
  comparator before the FG chain. Mode decides who drives the ladder — which is
  exactly Gap 5 ↓.

  Gap 2 — Hardware fix so it never clips ✓

  Don't aim for ±15V; aim for ±13V (safe margin under the ±13.5V TL071 ceiling).
  Just cap the maximum gain:

  $$G_\max = \frac{13}{2.5} = 5.2 ;\Rightarrow; \text{set } R_f = 51\text{k},;
  R_{g,\min}=10\text{k} ;\Rightarrow; G_\max = 5.1 ;\Rightarrow;
  \pm12.75\text{V}$$

  So change R25/R30 from 60k → 51k. Now even full-scale DAC at top gain hits
  ±12.75V — physically cannot clip. No software clamp needed (hardware
  guarantees it). Lower amplitude ranges use larger Rg.

  Gap 3 — Driving 50Ω: how to fix

  The op-amp alone can't. Three options, cheap → clean:

  1. Discrete push-pull buffer inside the feedback loop (complementary pair,
  e.g. BD139/BD140 or TIP31/TIP32, biased Class-AB) — the op-amp drives the
  transistor bases, feedback taken from the emitter output. Boosts current to
  ±0.5–1A. ~₹50 of parts. Recommended for a campus build.
  2. BUF634 buffer chip inside the loop — clean, ±250mA, drop-in.
  Costlier/harder to source.
  3. Leave it high-Z only + the 50Ω series (R35) for cable matching. Into a 50Ω
  terminated load you get half voltage and the op-amp still can't supply it — so
  this only works for scope/1MΩ loads.

  For a function generator that drives real 50Ω gear, do option 1 on the final
  output stage. Want me to add the push-pull buffer to the design?

  Gap 4 — You're right, I was wrong-ish. Let me re-explain.

  Your model: DAC always full 16-bit, scale in analog. That's actually the
  better choice for waveform shape — the shape is always drawn with all 65536
  levels, so it's always smooth, no matter the amplitude. 👍

  What I meant only applies if you scale digitally (shrink the wave inside the
  DAC) — then a small amplitude uses few codes and the shape gets coarse. Since
  you're scaling in analog, that problem doesn't exist. Gap 4 is dissolved by
  your approach. ✅

  The one consequence: amplitude is now set by the analog gain, so amplitude
  steps are discrete (whatever values the gain bank gives — e.g.
  ±2.5/5/10/12.5V). Question for you: is discrete amplitude fine, or do you want
  fine/continuous amplitude? If fine, we add either more gain steps or a fine
  analog attenuator — but tell me how fine you need (e.g. 0.1V steps?).

  Gap 5 — DAC sharing, explained simply

  Think of the R-2R ladder as one pen:

  - The function generator uses the pen to draw the waveform (sends sample after
  sample).
  - The SAR oscilloscope uses the same pen to guess-and-check voltages (binary
  search) against the probe input.

  One pen can't draw and guess at the same instant:

  ┌───────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Mode    │  Who holds the   │                Scope uses                │
  │           │       pen        │                                          │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ FG only   │ FG draws         │ —                                        │
  │ (m0)      │                  │                                          │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ OSC only  │ FG idle → pen    │ 16-bit SAR (full precision, reuses the   │
  │ (m1)      │ free             │ ladder) ✅                               │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ BOTH (m2) │ FG is drawing →  │ internal 10-bit ADC (PA0) — independent, │
  │           │ pen busy         │  doesn't need the pen                    │
  └───────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So your "reuse the same ladder" works perfectly in OSC-only mode (full 16-bit
  scope). In BOTH mode the ladder is busy generating, so the scope quietly
  switches to the chip's built-in 10-bit ADC. That's all Gap 5 means — it's not
  a problem, just a documented fallback. Does that make sense now?

  Gap 6 — Slew rate: you're fine with it ✅

❯ here dotn we need a demux? Signal R-2R DAC (0–5V, always full 16-bit)         
       │                                                                        
       ├──────────────────────────────► SAR comparator reference (oscilloscope) 
       │                                                                        
     U7  center + unity  → ±2.5V (centered on 0)                                
       │                                                                        
                                                                                

● Good catch — there is a real issue at that split, but the fix is a buffer, not
  a demux. Let me explain both why no demux, and what you actually need.

  Why no demux

  A voltage node naturally fans out to many loads at once. The DAC's 0–5V can
  feed the SAR comparator and U7 simultaneously — both are high-impedance taps,
  so they don't "steal" the signal from each other. A demux physically routes a
  signal to one of N places; you'd only need that if the two destinations
  couldn't coexist. Here they can. Mode selection is done in firmware (what it 
  reads/enables), not by physically rerouting the DAC:

  ┌──────┬────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Mode │ DAC driven │  Comparator (PD2)   │          FG output           │
  │      │     by     │                     │                              │
  ├──────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ FG   │ waveform   │ ignored             │ active                       │
  ├──────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ OSC  │ SAR search │ read                │ muted (open all gain-bank    │
  │      │            │                     │ switches — free)             │
  ├──────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ BOTH │ waveform   │ — (use internal     │ active                       │
  │      │            │ 10-bit ADC)         │                              │
  └──────┴────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  So muting the FG output in OSC mode costs nothing — firmware just opens the
  CD4066 gain switches. No hardware routing.

  The real issue you sensed — current-mode kills the voltage node ⚠️

  Here's the subtle trap. If the R-2R ladder feeds U7's virtual ground directly
  (the elegant current-mode DAC we discussed), then the node sits at 0V —
  there's no 0–5V voltage there for the SAR comparator to read. The comparator
  needs the actual DAC voltage.

  Fix: add a unity voltage-follower right after the ladder:

  R-2R DAC (Voc, 10k Thévenin)
     │
   [BUFFER]  unity voltage-follower (1 spare TL071 section)
     │   clean low-impedance 0–5V
     ├──────────────► SAR comparator reference   (high-Z tap)
     │
     └──[10k]──► U7 (center + gain)              (now a normal input resistor)

  The buffer gives a solid 0–5V voltage that both paths can tap without loading
  each other. This is the standard way to share an R-2R output. Costs one op-amp
  section (you have spare TL071s).

  ▎ Side effect: U7 now uses a real 10k input resistor after the buffer, instead
  ▎ of the ladder's Thévenin. Same gain math (−R7/10k), just an explicit 
  ▎ resistor.

  When you would use a demux

  If you didn't want to buffer, the alternative is an analog switch/demux
  routing the ladder to U7 in FG mode and to the comparator in OSC mode. But
  that's worse: more switching, it still can't serve both in BOTH mode, and it
  adds a part in the precision DAC path. The buffer is cleaner and always-on.

  Recommendation: one voltage-follower after the ladder, no demux. Want me to
  add it to the architecture in the docs?

❯ one voltage-follower after the ladder then. so then U& does centerign and no

● Correct on both counts.

  Yes — one voltage-follower after the ladder

  R-2R DAC ──► [Voltage Follower] ──► clean 0–5V ──┬──► SAR comparator (scope)
                                                    └──[10k]──► U7

  Yes — U7 does the centering, no centering IC

  U7 is an inverting summer with two inputs:
  - the buffered DAC signal (via 10k)
  - the −8V rail (via 32k)

  $$V_1 = -\frac{R_7}{10\text k}V_s + \frac{R_7}{32\text k}\cdot 8 = -(V_s -
  2.5)$$

  The 32k resistor subtracts the 2.5V midpoint → output centered on 0V. That
  single resistor is the centering. So:

  - ❌ U23 (CD4066 centering bank + R31–R34) → removed
  - ✅ Centering = one 32k resistor into U7
  - ✅ Drift/tolerance trimmed by the offset DAC in software (per-gain
  calibration)

  So U7 now does two jobs in one stage

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │   U7 input   │     resistor     │           job           │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ buffered DAC │ 10k (R7 partner) │ the signal              │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ −8V rail     │ 32k (R_cen)      │ centering (−2.5V shift) │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ feedback     │ R7 = 10k         │ unity gain              │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  One op-amp, two resistors, fully centered. Same applies to U9 on the offset
  path (10k + 32k from −8V).

  Net component change at the input: +1 buffer op-amp (shared by scope + FG),
  +1×32k per path, −1 whole CD4066 + 4 resistors (U23 bank). You come out ahead
  on part count and the scope-sharing works cleanly.

  Want me to write this finalized architecture (buffer + U7/U9 centering, U23
  removed, 51k gain, gain-after-mux) into the docs now?

❯ centerign apth?                                                               

● Right — let me clarify what "centering" is now, because it's no longer a path,
  just a resistor.

  Old way (removed): centering was a whole path

  −8V → U23 (CD4066 bank, 4 switches) → R31–R34 → into U16 summing node

  That was a separate switched signal path feeding the final summing amp. Gone.

  New way: centering is just one resistor inside U7 (and U9)

  There is no separate centering path anymore. The −8V rail injects a fixed
  current into the existing U7 summing node through a single 32k resistor:

  SIGNAL path:
    buffered DAC ──[10k]──┐
                          ├──► U7 (─) ──► centered ±2.5V signal
    −8V ─────────[32k]────┘        (R7=10k feedback)
                  ↑
            this resistor = "centering"

  OFFSET path:
    buffered offset DAC ──[10k]──┐
                                 ├──► U9 (─) ──► centered ±2.5V offset
    −8V ────────────────[32k]────┘        (R13=10k feedback)
                         ↑
                   centering for offset

  So:
  - Both paths get centered — the signal at U7, the offset at U9 — each with its
  own 32k from −8V.
  - "Centering path" = just the −8V → 32k → op-amp inverting node connection.
  Two resistors total (one per op-amp), no IC, no switches.
  - The −8V is one shared internal rail feeding both 32k resistors.

  Is that what you were asking — where the centering connects? Or did you mean
  whether the offset path also needs its own centering (answer: yes, U9 gets a
  32k too, so offset is bipolar ±2.5V before its gain)?

❯ ah i forgoth i need fien adjustments alognisde my 8 bit amplitide contellier  
  The one consequence: amplitude is now set by the analog gain, so amplitude    
  steps are discrete (whatever values the gain bank                             
    gives — e.g. ±2.5/5/10/12.5V). Question for you: is discrete amplitude

● Important flag first: an 8-bit binary-weighted switched-resistor gain bank is 
  non-monotonic with real resistors (the MSB transition flips the output the
  wrong way, and calibration can't fix non-monotonicity). So however we do
  "8-bit amplitude," it must be a monotonic method. Here are the clean ways to
  get coarse + fine amplitude, all monotonic:

  Option A — Coarse analog octaves + fine digital vernier (no extra parts) ⭐

  - Coarse: gain bank as a few octave ranges (×0.625 / ×1.25 / ×2.5 / ×5) — 1
  CD4066
  - Fine: scale the wavetable only within 50–100% (one octave) in firmware
  - Because you never go below 50%, you always use ≥ 32 768 codes → ≥15-bit 
  shape, and scaling stays symmetric around 0x8000, so centering still works
  automatically
  - Result: continuous fine amplitude, monotonic, zero extra hardware

  This honors your "full DAC" rule in spirit — the DAC is never below
  half-scale, so shape quality is essentially untouched.

  Option B — Coarse analog + 8-bit digital potentiometer vernier ⭐ (if DAC must
  stay literally 100%)

  - Coarse: gain bank octave ranges
  - Fine: an 8-bit digital pot (256 taps, SPI) as a variable gain on the bipolar
  ±2.5V signal — needs a dual-supply/±-capable pot
  - Digital pots are a tapped resistor string → inherently monotonic, 256 steps
  - DAC stays at full 16-bit always; fine amplitude is true analog
  - Cost: one extra SPI chip

  Option C — R-2R Multiplying DAC (your R-2R approach, all-analog)

  - Build a second R-2R where the reference = the signal and the digital code
  sets attenuation (0…1)
  - Monotonic (R-2R), 8-bit or more, reuses your R-2R skill
  - Cost: more wiring (another ladder + switches)

  ❌ Avoid

  8-bit binary switched-resistor gain bank — non-monotonic, calibration can't
  save it.

  ---
  My recommendation: Option A. It gives you continuous fine amplitude with no
  extra parts, stays monotonic, and keeps the DAC ≥50% (shape unaffected). The
  coarse CD4066 octaves are the "8-bit-ish controller" and the digital vernier
  fills between them seamlessly.

  If you insist the DAC must literally run 100% at all times, go Option B
  (digital pot).

  Which fits your intent — A (digital vernier, no parts) or B (digital pot, true
  analog fine)?

❯ how abotu A MOSFET push-pull current booster is a circuit that increases the  
  drive current capability of a control signal (like a PWM from an Arduino) to  
  rapidly switch heavier loads, minimizing power loss. i dotn liek bjt they     
  distorit signal i hope mosfet doesnt                                          

● Quick but important correction: the definition you pasted is for a switching
  booster (PWM, switch heavy loads on/off). Our output stage is a linear buffer
  — it has to faithfully reproduce the analog waveform, not switch. So the
  MOSFET's switching-speed advantage doesn't apply here, and the distortion
  question works differently than you'd expect.

  Where the distortion actually comes from

  Push-pull distortion is crossover distortion — the glitch at the zero-crossing
  where one device hands off to the other. It is not a BJT-vs-MOSFET thing.
  It's killed by two things:

  1. Class-AB bias (a couple of diodes / a Vbe-multiplier) so both devices stay
  slightly on through the handoff
  2. Putting the booster inside the op-amp feedback loop — the op-amp measures
  the output and corrects whatever nonlinearity the transistors add, including
  crossover

  With both, THD is very low whether you use BJT or MOSFET. The op-amp does the
  heavy lifting.

  The surprise: for linear use, BJT is usually cleaner than MOSFET

  This is the opposite of your assumption:

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │                        │         BJT         │ MOSFET (source follower) │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Turn-on threshold      │ ~0.6V, tight        │ Vgs ~2–4V, variable      │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Crossover region       │ small               │ larger → distorts more   │
  │                        │                     │ if underbiased           │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Transconductance at    │ high                │ low → worse near         │
  │ low current            │                     │ zero-crossing            │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Bias complexity        │ simple (2 diodes)   │ needs more headroom +    │
  │                        │                     │ care                     │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Thermal runaway        │ possible (needs     │ self-stabilizing (MOSFET │
  │                        │ emitter resistors)  │  plus)                   │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  So BJTs are what almost every linear audio output stage uses — they handle the
  zero-crossing better, not worse. MOSFETs can distort more if biased lazily.
  MOSFET's one real win here is thermal stability.

  What I'd actually recommend (cleanest, lowest distortion)

  Since you want minimal fuss and low distortion, skip discrete transistors
  entirely:

  - A buffer IC inside the loop (e.g. BUF634, ±250mA) — or a power op-amp
  (OPA548) as the final stage. One part, designed for low-distortion linear
  drive, handles your ±13V / ~250mA into 50Ω.
  - This is not a "controller/pot/switch" — it's an amplifier, fully compatible
  with your all-digital rule. Nothing to operate.

  If you insist on discrete MOSFET: complementary source-follower (e.g. a
  logic-level N/P pair), Class-AB biased, inside the op-amp loop. It'll work and
  won't distort if properly biased — but it's more finicky than the BJT version
  for no real benefit at audio frequencies.

  Bottom line: for a linear bench output at ≤20kHz, BJT-in-loop or a buffer IC
  gives lower distortion with less effort than MOSFET. Your "BJT distorts" worry
  only applies to an unbiased, open-loop stage — which we wouldn't build either
  way.

  Do you want (a) a buffer IC (BUF634/power op-amp, simplest), (b) discrete BJT
  in-loop, or (c) discrete MOSFET in-loop despite the above?

❯ what do you say. we can with yoru idea as well + use 16 bit ladder for slow   
  freq   In BOTH mode the signal ladder is busy generating, so the scope falls

● Right topology (complementary source-follower), but as drawn it has three 
  problems — and one of them is the dealbreaker for ±13V.

  Issues with the schematic as drawn

  1. No bias → Class B → huge dead zone. The gates are tied straight to the
  input. MOSFETs need Vgs ≈ 2–4V each to turn on, so there's a 4–8V dead band at
  the zero-crossing where neither conducts. That's massive crossover
  distortion. You must add a Class-AB bias spreader between the two gates (a
  Vbe-multiplier or diode/zener string) to pre-turn-on both devices.
  2. Single supply (V+ / Gnd). For your bipolar ±13V output the P-channel drain
  must go to −15V, not Gnd:
              +15V
               │ D
    gate_N ──G─┤ N-ch
               │ S
               ├──●── OUTPUT ──► (feedback to op-amp −)
               │ S
    gate_P ──G─┤ P-ch
               │ D
              −15V

  3. Must be inside the op-amp feedback loop (feedback taken from the OUTPUT
  node). Standalone it can't be accurate.

  The dealbreaker: MOSFET source-follower eats your swing 🔴

  A source-follower drops Vgs (~3–4V) between gate and output. On ±15V rails:

  - op-amp can drive the gate to ~+13.5V → output ≈ 13.5 − 4 = only ~±9–10V

  So a MOSFET buffer on ±15V gives you maybe ±10V, not ±13V. To recover ±13V
  you'd need ±18–20V gate-drive rails. A BJT emitter-follower only drops ~0.7V →
  keeps ~±13V on the same rails.

  So for ±13V on your existing ±15V rails, BJT (or a buffer IC) is the physical 
  winner. MOSFET costs you ~3V of output unless you raise the rails. If you're
  happy with ~±10V output, MOSFET is fine (biased + in-loop). Your call:
  - ±13V output → BJT-in-loop or BUF634
  - ~±10V output OK → MOSFET as you drew it, plus bias + −15V drain + in-loop

  Your other two points — both confirmed ✓

  Offset tradeoff: |offset| + amplitude ≤ 13V, amplify signal up to that. ✅
  Locked.

  Scope in BOTH mode — nice idea, and yes: at low FG frequency the DDS updates
  slowly, so you can time-share the ladder for 16-bit SAR between waveform
  updates (accepting a tiny FG output blip during each conversion). At high FG 
  frequency there's no time → fall back to the 10-bit internal ADC. Firmware
  picks based on frequency:

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BOTH mode   │                    Scope path                    │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Low FG freq  │ 16-bit SAR (ladder time-shared, minor FG glitch) │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ High FG freq │ 10-bit internal ADC                              │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ OSC-only     │ full 16-bit SAR, no glitch                       │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Confirm the swing/transistor choice (±13V-BJT vs ±10V-MOSFET) and I'll
  finalize all the docs.

● Good call — high-Z output only, skip the hard-to-find buffer ICs, and add
  fault protection. Here's the clean way to do it without touching your 
  beautiful signal.

  First: the TL071 already self-protects

  The TL071 has internal short-circuit current limiting (~25mA). A momentary
  short won't instantly kill it — it just clamps its own output current. So you
  already have a first line of defense. The protection below is the smart,
  layered second line.

  Protection that keeps the signal path pristine

  1. Series output resistor — passive limit (in path, but just a resistor → no 
  distortion)
  A ~100–220Ω series resistor at the output:
  - Into your high-Z loads (scope 1MΩ) → negligible drop, signal untouched
  - On a dead short → limits current to 13V/220Ω ≈ 60mA (and the op-amp's
  internal limit caps it lower anyway)
  - Also isolates the op-amp from cable capacitance/ESD

  2. Rail clamp diodes at the output node (output → +15V, output → −15V, fast
  diodes e.g. 1N4148/BAV199)
  - Catches externally-injected over-voltage (someone connects another source to
  your FG output) — shunts it to the rails before it reaches the op-amp.

  3. Smart current sense → MCU (out of the signal path) ⭐
  Put a small sense resistor in the output op-amp's ±15V supply line, not the
  signal line — so it never touches the waveform:
  +15V ──[R_sense ~1Ω]──► output op-amp V+ ──► (drives output)
                │
           [comparator]  trips when supply current > ~30mA
                │
            MCU pin (interrupt)
  On trip, firmware (all digital, your rule):
  - Mutes the output by opening the gain-bank CD4066 switches (no extra hardware
  — reuses what's there)
  - Lights a fault LED
  - Sends a fault message over Bluetooth
  - Latches off until you send a reset command

  Sensing supply current = the signal path stays a clean resistor + clamps only.
  The "beautiful output" is never degraded by the protection.

  Why this matches your design

  - All digital: the trip is sensed by hardware (fast) but the
  shutdown/mute/report is done by the MCU — no manual switch, no pot.
  - Layered: op-amp internal limit (instant) → series R + clamps (passive) → MCU
  mute + alert (smart, ms).
  - Signal-clean: only a series resistor + reverse-biased clamp diodes are in
  the path; the current sense is on the supply rail.

  Want me to add this output protection block (series R, rail clamps,
  supply-side current sense → MCU fault input) into REQUIREMENTS.md and
  CIRCUIT_CHANGES.md, along with everything else we've settled (buffer → U7
  centering → filter → mux → 51k coarse gain + digital fine, U23 removed)?

● Decisions are essentially complete — nothing major left open. Let me confirm
  the full settled list, set two trivial defaults myself, then write everything
  into the docs (that's the only action still pending).

  All settled ✅

  - Two 16-bit DACs kept; fully digital control (no pots/switches/controllers)
  - Reorder: buffer → U7 centering → Sallen-Key → CD4051 mux → coarse gain →
  output (±15V never hits the ±8V mux)
  - Voltage follower after the signal ladder (shared with scope comparator)
  - Centering = single 32k from −8V at U7/U9; U23 bank removed
  - Amplitude = CD4066 coarse octaves + digital wavetable fine (≥50%); R25/R30 =
  51k → ±13V, no clipping
  - Offset = mirror path, |offset|+amp ≤ 13V
  - Output = high-Z only; protection = series R + rail clamps + supply-side
  current sense → MCU mute/fault/Bluetooth, latched
  - Scope: OSC-only = 16-bit SAR (reuses ladder); BOTH = 16-bit SAR at low FG
  freq, 10-bit ADC at high freq

  Two trivial things I'm deciding for you (note if you disagree)

  1. Follower only on the signal ladder — the offset ladder feeds U9 in
  current-mode (not shared with the scope), so it needs no follower.
  2. Old PORTC 8-bit DAC nets (leftover DAC0808 plan) → mark for removal;
  doesn't touch the 16-bit DACs.

  (Offset-DAC pin assignment + current-sense threshold are wiring-time details,
  not design decisions.)

      140 -                                                              
      141 -- **16-bit:** 5V / 65536 = 76 µV/step → you can land on exact 
          -round numbers (10 mV, 100 mV,                                 
      142 -  1.000 V) by choosing the nearest code. Clean "increment by 1
          -0" is possible.                                               
      143 -- **8-bit:** 5V / 256 = 19.5 mV/step → cannot hit 10 mV; neare
          -st is 19.5 mV or 39 mV. Round                                 
      144 -  increments are impossible.                                  
      145 -                                                              
      146 -This is the reason **both the signal and offset DACs remain 16
          --bit** — it is what makes

❯ how to do tell me Offset-DAC latch pin (share MOSI/SCK, pick a spare latch pin) 
    - Current-sense trip threshold (~30mA starting point)                         
    - Fault-input pin (suggested PD3)                                             

● Here's the concrete "how to" for each — and note point 1 changed since you're
  now bit-banging the offset DAC (no MOSI/SCK sharing).

  1. Offset-DAC drive — bit-bang on 3 free pins

  Free pins (from your netlist, PA5/PA6/PA7 are unconnected): use them as DATA /
  CLOCK / LATCH.

  Wiring (offset 74HC595 pair, daisy-chained like the signal DAC):
  PA5 → DS (pin 14, IC1)        IC1 QH' → IC2 DS  (daisy chain)
  PA6 → SRCLK (pin 11, both)    SRCLR (pin 10) → +5V
  PA7 → RCLK  (pin 12, both)    OE    (pin 13) → GND

  Firmware (bit-bang, MSB first — speed irrelevant since offset rarely changes):
  void offset_dac_write(uint16_t val) {
      for (int8_t i = 15; i >= 0; i--) {
          if (val & (1u << i)) PORTA |=  (1<<PA5);
          else                 PORTA &= ~(1<<PA5);
          PORTA |=  (1<<PA6);   // clock high
          PORTA &= ~(1<<PA6);   // clock low
      }
      PORTA |=  (1<<PA7);       // latch
      PORTA &= ~(1<<PA7);
  }

  2. Current-sense trip threshold — how to set it

  Threshold value: your high-Z loads draw <1mA normally; the TL071 internally
  limits at ~25mA. So trip below that — ~15–20mA — to shut down before the
  op-amp is even stressed. (30mA is OK as a ceiling, but 15–20mA is safer for
  "don't harm my outputs".)

  Two ways to build it:

  (a) Simplest — sense resistor + transistor detector (hardware trip, ~2 parts):
  R_s in the output op-amp's +15V feed.
  R_s = 0.6V / I_trip = 0.6 / 0.02 = 30Ω  (PNP turns on when I·R_s > 0.6V)
  PNP across R_s → collector signals fault (needs level-shift to 0–5V for the
  MCU).

  (b) Recommended — high-side current-sense amp → MCU (fits "all digital"):
  INA181 (or ZXCT1009) high-side sense across R_s (e.g. 1Ω) in the +15V feed
   → outputs a ground-referenced 0–5V ∝ output current
   → into an MCU ADC pin   (MCU reads ACTUAL current, threshold set in software)
   → and into a comparator (LM393) for an instant hardware trip
  Option (b) lets you set/adjust the threshold in firmware, read the real
  current value, and report it over Bluetooth — and gives a fast hardware trip
  too. R_s = 1Ω → 20mV/20mA into the sense amp; tiny drop, tiny power (0.4mW).

  3. Fault-input pin — use PD3 = INT1

  PD3 is INT1 (external interrupt 1) on the ATmega32A → perfect for an instant
  fault trip.

  Wiring: detector/comparator output (open-collector, active-low) → PD3, with a
  pull-up to +5V.

  Firmware:
  // setup: PD3 input + pull-up, INT1 on falling edge
  DDRD  &= ~(1<<PD3);
  PORTD |=  (1<<PD3);
  MCUCR |= (1<<ISC11);          // INT1 falling edge
  GICR  |= (1<<INT1);           // enable INT1

  ISR(INT1_vect) {              // fault!
      mute_output();            // open all gain-bank CD4066 switches
      led_fault_on();
      uart_puts("FAULT: overcurrent\r\n");
      fault_latched = 1;        // stay off until reset command
  }

  Use INT0/PD2 is already the SAR comparator, so INT1/PD3 is the right free 
  choice.

  ---
  Summary of pin assignments now:

  ┌─────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │     Pin     │            Use            │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PB5/PB7/PB3 │ signal DAC — hardware SPI │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PA5/PA6/PA7 │ offset DAC — bit-bang     │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PD2 (INT0)  │ SAR comparator            │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PD3 (INT1)  │ overcurrent fault input   │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PA0         │ internal 10-bit ADC       │
  └─────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  Want me to start the firmware (offset DAC bit-bang driver + fault ISR +
  amplitude/offset coordination), or are you still on the schematic?

  The only thing I suggested that's not commonly available in Sri Lanka was the

  Overcurrent trip — Sri Lanka parts only

  Best option: sense resistor + PC817 optocoupler (PC817 is everywhere in SL,
  and the opto isolates so there's no ±15V level-shift headache):

  +15V ──[R_s]──► output op-amp V+         R_s ≈ 47–68Ω, 0.25W
            │
            ├──[R_led ~100Ω]──►|── PC817 LED (anode to op-amp side)
            │                          across R_s
     PC817 transistor: collector → PD3 (pull-up to +5V), emitter → GND

  How it trips: normal high-Z current is <1mA → tiny drop across R_s → opto LED
  dark. On overload, I_out × R_s rises past the opto LED's ~1.2V → LED lights →
  PC817 transistor pulls PD3 low → MCU fault ISR.

  Threshold tuning: with R_s = 68Ω, the LED starts conducting around 1.2V / 68Ω 
  ≈ 18mA — right in your 15–20mA target. Raise R_s to trip earlier, lower to
  trip later.

  Sharper alternative: LM393 comparator (also common at tronic.lk) for a crisp
  threshold — but the opto is simpler and needs no high-side reference, so I'd
  use the PC817.

  Full BOM — all confirmed SL-available

❯ how the sharper alternative loo like with LM393                               

● Here's the LM393 version. The trick with high-side sensing is that the sense
  voltage sits up at +15V — outside the LM393's input range — so a PNP (BC557)
  first translates it down to a ground-referenced voltage, then the LM393 makes
  a sharp digital trip. All SL-available parts (BC557, LM393, resistors).

  LM393 overcurrent trip (sharp threshold)

          +15V
           │
          [R_s 30Ω]            ← sets trip ≈ 0.6V/30Ω = 20mA
           │
           ├───────────────────────► output op-amp V+
           │
         emitter
         BC557 (PNP)
           │ base ──[Rb 1k]──● (op-amp side of R_s)
         collector
           │
          [R_c 10k]
           │
         node A ─────────────────► LM393  IN−
           │
          GND

     +5V ──[R1 47k]──┬──[R2 4.7k]── GND
                     │
                  node B (~0.45V) ─► LM393  IN+      (threshold)

     LM393 powered from +5V.
     LM393 output (open-collector) ──[pull-up 10k → +5V]──► PD3 (INT1,
  active-LOW fault)

  How it works:
  - The PNP's base–emitter sees the drop across R_s. When I_out × R_s approaches
  ~0.6V (≈20mA), the BC557 turns on and pushes current through R_c → node A
  rises (now ground-referenced, 0–few V, safely inside the LM393's +5V input
  range).
  - LM393 compares node A (IN−) against the ~0.45V threshold (IN+). On
  overcurrent, IN− > IN+ → output goes LOW → pulls PD3 low → fault ISR.
  - LM393 runs on +5V, so its output is already MCU-safe (0–5V) — no extra level
  shift.

  Tuning:
  - Trip current = 0.6V / R_s → 30Ω = 20mA, 47Ω = 13mA, 22Ω = 27mA.
  - The R1/R2 threshold just sets how far node A must rise before it latches the
  edge — keeps it from chattering near the onset.

  LM393 vs PC817 — which to use

  ┌─────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │                     │      PC817 opto      │        LM393 + BC557        │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Parts               │ 1 opto + 2 R         │ 1 PNP + 1 comparator + ~5 R │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Level-shift         │ automatic (isolated) │ PNP does it                 │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Threshold sharpness │ soft (LED knee)      │ sharp                       │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ Simplicity          │ simpler              │ a bit more                  │
  └─────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  Both are fine and both are SL-stocked. PC817 = fewer parts; LM393 = crisper 
  trip. For a clean, well-defined cutoff I'd lean LM393; for minimum parts,
  PC817.

  Want me to put one of these (your pick) into CIRCUIT_CHANGES.md as the final
  protection circuit, then move to firmware?

✻ Cogitated for 1m 33s

❯ which one do nto harm the signal output?                                      

● Neither harms the signal — and that's by design. Both versions sense current
  on the +15V supply rail feeding the op-amp, not on the signal path. The
  waveform never passes through the sense resistor, so neither can distort it.

  What's actually in your signal path:
  - the series output resistor (100–220Ω) — a plain linear resistor, zero
  distortion, negligible drop into high-Z loads
  - the rail clamp diodes — reverse-biased and silent in normal operation; they
  only conduct on an external over-voltage fault

  The sense resistor sits off to the side, in the power feed:
  +15V ──[R_s]──► op-amp V+        ← sensing happens HERE (supply)
                    │
                    └──► op-amp output ──[R_out]──► BNC   ← signal path: only a
  clean resistor

  The only difference between them

  R_s in the supply causes a tiny supply voltage drop (not a signal drop).
  Smaller R_s = less headroom lost:

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │             │   sense R needed    │    supply drop @     │   signal    │
  │             │                     │      quiescent       │   effect    │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ LM393 +     │ 30Ω (PNP trips at   │ ~0.15V               │ none        │
  │ BC557       │ 0.6V)               │                      │             │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ PC817 opto  │ 68Ω (LED needs      │ ~0.3V                │ none        │
  │             │ ~1.2V)              │                      │             │
  └─────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  So LM393 is marginally gentler (smaller sense resistor → less rail drop). But
  both drops are trivial: even 0.3V off the +15V rail still leaves ~14.7V, and
  you only need to swing to ±13V — so no impact on your output either way.

  Verdict

  → LM393 + BC557 if you want the absolute least supply-rail intrusion and a
  sharp trip.
  → PC817 if you want fewer parts.

  Either way your "beautiful output" is untouched — the protection lives on the
  power side, and the signal path stays a clean resistor.

  Shall I lock in the LM393 version in CIRCUIT_CHANGES.md and move to firmware?

❯ si the transsitor circuti okay nor heatign issyes?                            

● No heating issues anywhere in the sense circuit — everything runs in
  milliwatts. Here are the actual numbers:

  ┌─────────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────────┬────────────┬───────┐
  │  Component  │  Worst-case current  │    Power    │  Rating    │ Hot?  │
  │             │                      │             │   needed   │       │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ R_s (30Ω)   │ 25mA (op-amp's own   │ 0.025²×30 = │ 0.25W (8×  │ ❄️    │
  │             │ limit during short)  │  19mW       │ margin)    │ cold  │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ BC557       │ Ic limited to ~1.5mA │ Vce×Ic ≈    │ 500mW      │ ❄️    │
  │             │  by R_c              │ <10mW       │ device     │ cold  │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ R_c, Rb,    │ µA–mA                │ <1mW        │ 0.25W      │ ❄️    │
  │ R1, R2      │                      │             │            │ cold  │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼───────┤
  │ LM393       │ quiescent ~1mA       │ ~5mW        │ —          │ ❄️    │
  │             │                      │             │            │ cold  │
  └─────────────┴──────────────────────┴─────────────┴────────────┴───────┘

  The BC557 can't overheat because R_c (10k) limits its collector current to 
  ~1.5mA — there's simply no path for big current through it. The base is
  protected by Rb (1k). It's a sensing transistor, not a power transistor — it
  never carries the load.

  The one thing that does heat — and why the circuit exists

  During a short, the output op-amp itself dissipates 15V × 25mA ≈ 375mW
  internally (within the TL071's ~680mW limit, but it gets warm if sustained).
  That's exactly what the trip prevents — it mutes within milliseconds, so the
  op-amp never sits in a sustained short. The protection circuit's whole job is
  to stop that heating.

  One sizing note (not the transistors)

  The series output resistor R_out (100–220Ω) briefly carries short current
  before the trip fires:
  - 100Ω → 0.025²×100 = 62mW → 0.25W fine
  - 220Ω → 138mW → use 0.5W for margin

  Everything else stays cold and needs no heatsinks. The transistor circuit is
  completely safe thermally — and it actively saves the op-amp from the only
  real heat source.

  Lock in the LM393 protection and move to firmware?

❯ i liek │ LM393 + BC557  way                                                   

❯ but nee absolutely shure thsi si the best way to habdl ethe trnasistor        
● Honest answer: the single-rail version I showed is good but not complete — and
  since you asked to be absolutely sure, here's the gap and the genuinely best
  way.

  The flaw in single-rail sensing 🔴

  The BC557 on the +15V rail only sees current when the op-amp sources current —
  i.e. on a positive-output short. But your output swings ±13V continuously. If
  the output is in its negative half and gets shorted, the op-amp sinks that
  current into the −15V rail — which never flows through the +15V sense 
  resistor. So a single +15V PNP misses every negative-output fault.

  For real protection you must sense both rails.

  The best way — dual-rail sense (PNP + NPN) into the dual LM393

  The LM393 has two comparators in one chip — perfect, one per rail:

   +15V ──[Rs1 30Ω]──┬──► op-amp V+
                     │
              e  BC557 (PNP)          ← catches POSITIVE-output faults
              b ─[1k]─ (op-amp side)
              c ─[10k]→ GND ──► node A ──► LM393-A
                                           │
                                           ├─(open-collector outputs)
   op-amp V− ──┬──[Rs2 30Ω]── −15V        │  wire-OR'd together
               │                           │
          e  BC547 (NPN)                    ──► PD3 (INT1, active-LOW)
          b ─[1k]─ (op-amp side)           │     pull-up 10k → +5V
          c ─[10k]→ GND ──► node B ──► LM393-B
                                  ↑ catches NEGATIVE-output faults

  - BC557 (PNP) on +15V trips when sourcing current × Rs1 > 0.6V
  - BC547 (NPN) on −15V trips when sinking current × Rs2 > 0.6V
  - Both LM393 outputs are open-collector → just tie them together → either
  fault pulls PD3 low
  - BC557 + BC547 are a standard complementary pair — both stocked at tronic.lk
  / nilabra

  Why this is the correct way to "handle the transistor"

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Concern       │                      Answer                       │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Both fault          │ ✅ PNP catches +, NPN catches −                   │
  │ polarities          │                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Transistor heating  │ ✅ R_c (10k) limits collector to ~1.5mA → <10mW,  │
  │                     │ cold                                              │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Base safety         │ ✅ Rb (1k) limits base current                    │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Uses parts          │ ✅ one dual LM393 does both rails                 │
  │ efficiently         │                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Threshold drift     │ Vbe ~2mV/°C → trip point moves ~3% over temp —    │
  │                     │ fine for protection                               │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SL availability     │ ✅ BC557, BC547, LM393 all common                 │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  This dual-rail PNP/NPN scheme is the textbook way to protect a bipolar (±) 
  output stage — it's what bench supplies use. The single-rail version would
  leave half your waveform unprotected.

  This is the best way. Want me to lock the dual-rail LM393 + BC557/BC547
  protection into CIRCUIT_CHANGES.md and move on?

❯ will thsi harm the signal or sine the new part can other way handl ejust fine 
  just askign to know?                                                          

● You're asking the right question. Honest answer in two parts:

  Will the supply-rail sense harm the signal?

  Practically, no — for your high-impedance output it's negligible. Here's the
  actual physics:

  - The sense resistors are in the supply rails, not the signal path
  - The only effect is a tiny signal-dependent supply droop (I × Rs varies as
  the output current varies), which the op-amp's PSRR rejects (~90dB)
  - For high-Z loads the output current is µA–mA, so that droop is sub-millivolt
  — far below anything you'd see on your output

  So it doesn't meaningfully touch your "beautiful signal." The effect only
  grows at high load current, which you don't have.

  Can it be handled another way? Yes — and the simplest way touches the signal 
  least

  Here's the thing most people forget: the TL071 already has built-in 
  short-circuit protection. It self-limits its output at ~25mA and survives a
  short to ground. So you have options:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │          Approach          │   Signal     │      Protection level      │
  │                            │    impact    │                            │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1. Rely on TL071's         │              │ survives shorts (op-amp    │
  │ internal limit + series    │ zero added   │ warms if sustained ~325mW, │
  │ R_out + clamp diodes       │              │  within rating)            │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2. Supply-rail PNP/NPN     │ negligible   │ fast mute + Bluetooth      │
  │ sense + LM393              │ (sub-mV)     │ fault + prevents sustained │
  │                            │              │  heating                   │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3. Diff-amp across R_out   │ negligible   │ both polarities, one point │
  │                            │              │  — but most parts          │
  └────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Option 1 is genuinely viable: the op-amp won't blow from an accidental short
  because it limits itself. You'd skip the whole sense circuit and just keep the
  passive series resistor + clamp diodes. The only downside is the op-amp gets
  warm during a sustained short (it doesn't die).

  Option 2 (what we designed) adds the smart bits you asked for — cut the 
  circuit when it senses high current, fault LED, Bluetooth alert — and prevents
  that sustained warming. Its signal cost is negligible.

  My honest recommendation

  If your worry is signal purity above all, Option 1 is the cleanest (zero added
  parts near the signal, op-amp self-protects). If you want the active shutdown
  + fault reporting you described earlier, Option 2 gives it with negligible
  signal cost.

  You don't lose signal quality either way — so pick based on whether you want
  the smart fault feature (→ Option 2) or maximum simplicity (→ Option 1). Which
  fits your goal?
