❯ yes it does see cleatly for "U30's SER (pin 14) must connect to U29's QH′     
  (pin 9) " yes for "Confirm SRCLK/RCLK reach both ICs                          
  " yes test passed for "Verify monotonic output with a ramp" i just want to    
  chekc the LSB and MSB positions.[Image #66]                                   
  ⎿  [Image #66]

● Since the ramp test passed monotonic, your MSB/LSB mapping is already correct
  — that's actually the proof. If MSB/LSB were swapped, the ramp would jump
  around instead of climbing smoothly. But let me show you exactly where each
  bit lands so you can see why:

  The bit positions

  Data flow: SPI is MSB-first, and in a daisy chain the first bit sent shifts 
  all the way through to the far chip. So:

  ┌──────────────┬───────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Code bit   │ Lands at  │             Ladder position              │
  ├──────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ bit 15 (MSB) │ U30 QH    │ nearest VOUT → highest weight            │
  ├──────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ bit 14…8     │ U30 QG…QA │ (high byte)                              │
  ├──────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ bit 7…1      │ U29 QH…QB │ (low byte)                               │
  ├──────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ bit 0 (LSB)  │ U29 QA    │ terminated end (R10→GND) → lowest weight │
  └──────────────┴───────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Why this is correct

  1. R-2R weighting: the node nearest VOUT = MSB (least attenuation); the node
  at the terminated end = LSB.
  2. Your VOUT is at the far right (past U30), termination R10 is at the far 
  left (U29 QA).
  3. MSB-first SPI: you send bit 15 first → it shifts through U29 → through QH′
  → all the way to U30 QH (the end nearest VOUT = highest weight). Last bit
  (bit 0) stays at U29 QA (terminated end = lowest weight).

  → MSB-data lands on the MSB-position, LSB-data on the LSB-position. Perfectly 
  aligned. ✓

  So, concretely

  - MSB = U30 QH (right side, nearest the buffer/VOUT) — U30 is the HIGH byte
  - LSB = U29 QA (left side, where R10→GND is) — U29 is the LOW byte

  (Note: this makes U29 the low byte even though it's first in the chain —
  that's normal for MSB-first + VOUT-at-far-end. Don't let the "first IC" fool
  you.)

  The clincher

  Your monotonic ramp test already confirms this is right. A correct ramp means
  every code increment raised the output by the proper weighted amount — which
  only happens if MSB→highest-weight node and LSB→lowest. So you're good. ✅

  If you ever want to double-check a specific bit: write 0x8000 (only MSB) →
  output should jump to half-scale (~2.35V at your 4.7V full-scale). Write
  0x0001 (only LSB) → output moves by one tiny step (~72µV). If those match,
  MSB/LSB are confirmed.

● Mostly correct — the mux logic is wired right, but there's one real issue to
  fix (logic levels), plus your naming question.

  Naming: yes, shorten it ✓

  IN_RAW is better than IN_RAW_MUX_A0. Hierarchical pin names should describe
  the signal's role inside the block, not the external net. Use:
  - IN_RAW, IN_FILT, MUX_OUT

  The external net (MUX_A0 etc.) is whatever you wire to the pin on the parent
  sheet — the pin name doesn't need to carry it. Clean and clear.

  ⚠️ The real issue: CD4051 logic levels at +8V

  Your CD4051 is powered VDD = +8V, but its select lines (A/B/C) are driven by
  the 74HC595 running on +5V. The CD4051's logic-high threshold at VDD=8V is VIH
  ≈ 0.7 × 8 ≈ 5.6V — but the 595 only outputs ~5V. So 5V may not be reliably 
  read as "high" → the mux could fail to switch channels (marginal,
  temperature/part dependent).

  Fix: power the CD4051 VDD at +5V (keep VEE = −8V for the negative signal
  swing):
  - Control logic threshold drops to ~3.5V → the 595's 5V is a solid "high" ✓
  - Signal range becomes −8V to +5V → your ±2.5V signal fits easily ✓
  - Valid supply (VDD−VEE = 13V, under the 20V max) ✓

  ▎ Same applies to your CD4066 gain banks if they're at +8V and driven by 5V 
  ▎ logic — check those too. All your analog switches handle only ≤±2.5V 
  ▎ signals, so VDD=+5V / VEE=−8V works for all of them.

  ✅ What's correct

  - QA/QB/QC → A/B/C select ✓ (QA alone toggles X0↔X1)
  - INH (pin 6) → GND = mux enabled ✓
  - Unused X2–X7 → GND ✓ (good practice)
  - X0 = raw, X1 = filtered, X(common) → MUX_OUT ✓

  🔍 Quick verify on U27 (595)

  - /SRCLR (pin 10) → +5V (tied high — else the register stays cleared and
  select is stuck at 0)
  - /OE (pin 13) → GND (outputs enabled)

  Confirm those two are tied correctly.

  Summary

  1. Use IN_RAW / IN_FILT / MUX_OUT — drop the verbose suffix ✓
  2. Change CD4051 VDD: +8V → +5V (keep VEE=−8V) — the important fix for
  reliable switching
  3. Verify /SRCLR→+5V and /OE→GND on the 595
  4. Mux topology itself is correct

  Still pending — the important one

  CD4051 VDD = +8V → change to +5V (keep VEE = −8V).

  Reason again: the 595 drives the mux select at 5V, but a +8V-powered CD4051
  needs ~5.6V to guarantee a logic "high" → marginal. Dropping VDD to +5V lowers

  - ⚠️ CD4051 VDD: +8V → +5V — still to do (and check your CD4066 gain banks for
  the same thing)

  Once the CD4051 VDD is on +5V, this whole mux + control block is correct.

  ⎿  [Image #69]

● Since timing's loose, here's my call:

  Don't put the mux 595 on the DAC SPI bus

  Keep PB5/PB7 (fast SPI) dedicated to the two DACs. The mux 595 is a control 
  register — it changes rarely (only when you switch filtered/raw), so it
  belongs on the control domain, not the hot 40kHz DAC bus. Mixing it onto
  PB5/PB7 would just add another device that shifts on every DDS sample for no
  reason.

  My recommendation: share SER + SRCLK across all control 595s, separate RCLK 
  each

  Treat the mux 595 like the other control shift registers (gain bank, etc.):

  ┌───────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Line  │                       Wire to                        │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SER   │ shared control-bus SER (common to all control 595s)  │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SRCLK │ shared control-bus SRCLK                             │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ RCLK  │ its own pin (so you can latch the mux independently) │
  └───────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Loose timing → sharing SER+SRCLK is totally fine, and you only spend one pin 
  per register (the latch).

  Even cleaner (most pin-efficient): daisy-chain all control 595s into one chain
  — SER→reg1 QH′→reg2 SER→…, shared SRCLK+RCLK, one latch for everything. Then
  3 pins drive all control. You shift the whole control word and latch once when
  changing mode. Since control changes are rare, re-shifting the whole chain
  costs nothing. The only downside is daisy-chaining QH′→SER across sub-sheets
  is slightly fiddly to wire.

  → For ease across your sub-sheets, go shared-SER+SRCLK / separate-RCLK. For
  absolute minimum pins, daisy-chain.

  ⚠️ One caution on pin choice

  PA0 is your scope's internal ADC input — don't reuse it for a 595 SER. Use the
  free pins instead: PB0, PB1, PB2, PB6 are open, or your existing PA/PD
  control pins (just not PA0).

  Concrete suggestion

  Dedicate a small control bus on free pins, e.g.:
  - CTRL_SER = PB0, CTRL_SRCLK = PB1 (shared by all control 595s)
  - MUX_RCLK = PB2 (mux latch), gain-bank latches on PB6 / spare PA-PD

  So: mux 595 → control bus (shared SER+SRCLK, own RCLK), NOT the DAC SPI. Loose
  timing makes this the simplest, cleanest split.

  PB3 → keep bare for all. Not required.

  2. Make the gain-bank 595 share the same control bus. Your signal gain bank
  (CD4066) is also driven by a 595 — wire its SER←CTRL_SER, SRCLK←CTRL_SRCLK
  (shared), and give it its own latch (e.g. GAIN_RCLK on free PB6 or a PA pin).
  That keeps all control registers on one shared bus with separate latches —
  consistent and pin-efficient.

  So: the OutputMux naming/connections are correct. Just reuse

  optionally tidy the mixed naming styles.

  DRC), so descriptive names make layout and debugging much easier. Worth the

  Pattern: PB<pin>_<block>_<signal>

  ┌─────┬─────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Pin │      Net name       │                Meaning                 │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PB5 │ PB5_DAC16_R2R_SER   │ DAC data — shared (signal + offset)    │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PB7 │ PB7_DAC16_R2R_SRCLK │ DAC clock — shared                     │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PB3 │ PB3_DAC16_SIG_RCLK  │ signal DAC latch                       │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PB4 │ PB4_DAC16_OFF_RCLK  │ offset DAC latch                       │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PB0 │ PB0_CTRL_SER        │ control-bus data — shared (mux + gain) │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PB1 │ PB1_CTRL_SRCLK      │ control-bus clock — shared             │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PB2 │ PB2_MUX_RCLK        │ mux 595 latch                          │
  ├─────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PB6 │ PB6_GAIN_RCLK       │ gain-bank 595 latch                    │
  └─────┴─────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The logic baked into the names

  - Shared lines carry the bus/block name → DAC16_R2R_SER, CTRL_SER (you
  instantly see "this fans out to multiple chips")
  - Per-device latches name the specific device → SIG, OFF, MUX, GAIN (you see
  exactly which chip it selects)
  - Pin prefix → trace straight back to the MCU on the PCB

  So on the board, PB3_DAC16_SIG_RCLK tells you everything: it's PB3, it's the
  DAC16, the signal one, its RCLK latch. Zero guessing during layout.

  Rename these three to match

  You currently have CTRL_SER, CTRL_SRCLK, MUX_RCLK (no pin prefix) and PB3/PB4
  (bare). To be fully consistent:
  - CTRL_SER → PB0_CTRL_SER
  - CTRL_SRCLK → PB1_CTRL_SRCLK
  - MUX_RCLK → PB2_MUX_RCLK
  - PB3 → PB3_DAC16_SIG_RCLK
  - PB4 → PB4_DAC16_OFF_RCLK

  (PB5/PB7 are already in the right style.)

  One practical note: keep names from getting too long or the PCB ratsnest
  labels get cramped — your ~17–18 char names are a good sweet spot.

❯ telel me hwo to rename                                                        

● Renaming labels in KiCad (Eeschema):

  The basic move

  1. Double-click the label text (or hover over it and press E)
  2. In the dialog, change the Label field to the new name
  3. Click OK

  That's it for one label.

  ⚠️ The key rule: rename EVERY copy of that net

  Labels connect by matching text, and each label is independent — editing one
  does not update the others. So for a net that has labels at several points,
  you must rename all of them to the identical new name, or the net splits in
  two.

  Example — CTRL_SER likely appears at 3 spots:
  - at the PB0 pin
  - at the OutputMux SER feed
  - at the gain-bank 595 SER feed

  → Edit all three to PB0_CTRL_SER. Miss one and you'll get a
  floating/half-connected net.

  What to rename (and what to leave alone)

  Rename these net labels (on the wires):

  ┌────────────┬────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │    Old     │        New         │    How many copies    │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ CTRL_SER   │ PB0_CTRL_SER       │ PB0 + every 595 SER   │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ CTRL_SRCLK │ PB1_CTRL_SRCLK     │ PB1 + every 595 SRCLK │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ MUX_RCLK   │ PB2_MUX_RCLK       │ PB2 + OutputMux RCLK  │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ PB3        │ PB3_DAC16_SIG_RCLK │ PB3 + signal DAC RCLK │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ PB4        │ PB4_DAC16_OFF_RCLK │ PB4 + offset DAC RCLK │
  └────────────┴────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  Leave alone — the sheet-pin names on the block (RCLK, SER, SRCLK on
  OutputMux). Those are the block's internal interface names; you only rename
  the net labels that connect to them.

  After renaming

  - Run Tools → ERC — it flags any label you missed (shows up as an unconnected
  or single-node net)
  - Use Inspect → Net highlight (or hover a wire) to confirm the renamed net
  spans all the right points

  Tip

  If you have many copies, hover + E is faster than double-click. KiCad has no
  "rename net everywhere" for labels — you edit each copy — so just be
  systematic: rename one, then immediately find and fix its siblings before
  moving on.

  The convention I'll now follow for every IC

  When discussing any IC in this design, I'll always give:

  1. Operating/supply voltage + a logic-level note (e.g. CD4051/CD4066 at +8V
  needs ~5.6V VIH → 5V logic is marginal → prefer +5V VDD)
  2. Pin table in this format:

  | Pin | Name | Type  | Connects to       |
  |-----|------|-------|-------------------|
  | 14  | SER  | Input | PB5_DAC16_R2R_SER |

  3. Electrical type per pin — Input / Output / Bidirectional / Power / Passive
  4. Hierarchical-sheet recommendation — whether it should be its own sub-sheet,
  with signal pins as hierarchical pins (IN/OUT) and power via global symbols

  Plus the net-naming convention PB<pin>_<BLOCK>_<SIGNAL> (descriptive,
  PCB-traceable).

  gain bank), I'll lay out its supply, full pin table with I/O directions, and

❯ [Image #75] oen more i saw one you said to set the +8V in theis mux to +5V    
  why? reasong teach em pleasE?why nto -8V kepeign safefe? even keepign unequla 
  voltages?                                                                     
  ⎿  [Image #75]

● Great question to dig into — this teaches an important concept: a CD4051 has 
  TWO separate electrical domains, and they care about different rails.

  The two domains inside the CD4051

  ┌──────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │    Domain    │       Pins        │ Referenced   │  What it cares about  │
  │              │                   │      to      │                       │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Digital      │ A, B, C (select), │ VDD − VSS    │ logic-high threshold  │
  │ control      │  INH              │              │ (VIH)                 │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Analog       │ X0–X7, X (common) │ VEE … VDD    │ signal voltage range  │
  │ signal       │                   │              │                       │
  └──────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  The select/enable pins are CMOS logic inputs; the X pins are the analog switch
  path. They're driven by different things and limited by different rails.

  Why VDD must be +5V (the logic-level reason) 🔴

  The CD4051's logic-high threshold is roughly:
  $$V_{IH} \approx 0.7 \times (V_{DD} - V_{SS})$$

  - At VDD = +8V: VIH ≈ 0.7 × 8 = 5.6V
  - Your 74HC595 runs on +5V, so its "high" output is only ~5.0V
  - 5.0V < 5.6V → the CD4051 may not recognize the 595's high as a high → the
  mux fails to switch reliably (and it's temperature/part dependent — works on
  the bench, fails later)

  Drop VDD to +5V:
  - VIH ≈ 0.7 × 5 = 3.5V
  - 595's 5V is now 1.5V above threshold → rock-solid "high" ✓

  So +8V doesn't make it "safer" — it actually breaks the control logic. The 595
  can't drive high enough. +5V is the correct choice, not a downgrade.

  Why KEEP VEE = −8V (don't touch it)

  VEE sets the negative limit of the analog signal, not the logic. Your signal
  through the mux swings to −2.5V, so you need VEE more negative than that. VEE 
  = −8V gives plenty of negative headroom — keep it. Changing VDD doesn't affect
  VEE's job.

  Why unequal (asymmetric) rails are totally fine

  The CD4051 has no requirement for symmetric supplies. It only needs:
  - VDD > VSS > VEE ✓ (+5 > 0 > −8)
  - analog signal stays within [VEE, VDD] → ±2.5V is inside [−8, +5] ✓
  - VDD − VEE ≤ 20V (abs max) → 5 − (−8) = 13V ✓

  So VDD = +5V, VEE = −8V, VSS = GND is perfectly valid and asymmetric is
  normal. The positive signal range (+5V) and negative range (−8V) don't have to
  match — your signal (±2.5V) fits in both directions.

  CD4051B — full spec (per our convention)

  Supply: VDD = +5V (was +8V — logic fix) · VEE = −8V · VSS = GND. Logic note: 
  VIH≈0.7·VDD; +5V VDD makes 74HC595's 5V a reliable high.

  ┌───────────────┬────────────┬───────────────┬───────────────┐
  │      Pin      │    Name    │     Type      │  Connects to  │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 16            │ VDD        │ Power         │ +5V           │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 7             │ VEE        │ Power         │ −8V           │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 8             │ VSS        │ Power         │ GND           │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 11            │ A          │ Input         │ 595 QA        │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 10            │ B          │ Input         │ 595 QB        │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 9             │ C          │ Input         │ 595 QC        │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 6             │ INH        │ Input         │ GND (enabled) │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 13            │ X0         │ Bidirectional │ IN_RAW        │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 14            │ X1         │ Bidirectional │ IN_FILT       │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 12,15,1,5,2,4 │ X2–X7      │ Bidirectional │ GND (unused)  │
  ├───────────────┼────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 3             │ X (common) │ Bidirectional │ MUX_OUT       │
  └───────────────┴────────────┴───────────────┴───────────────┘

  Hierarchical: lives in the OutputMux sub-sheet with the 595; signal pins
  (IN_RAW/IN_FILT/MUX_OUT) as hierarchical pins, power (+5V/−8V/GND) via global
  symbols.

  TL;DR

  - VDD = +5V because the 595 logic is 5V and a +8V CD4051 needs ~5.6V to read
  "high" → +8V breaks switching, +5V fixes it
  - VEE = −8V stays — it's for the negative signal swing (−2.5V), unaffected by
  the logic issue
  - Asymmetric +5/−8 is valid — CD4051 doesn't need matched rails, signal just
  has to fit inside [VEE, VDD]

  So change only VDD (+8→+5); keep VEE at −8V. Same applies to your CD4066 gain 
  bank (5V logic → use +5V VDD there too).

  Make a reused Centering sub-sheet

  Same approach as DAC16_R2R — one .kicad_sch, instantiated twice:

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │ Sheet name (instance) │        File         │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ CenterSignal          │ centering.kicad_sch │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ CenterOffset          │ centering.kicad_sch │

  KiCad auto-annotates the internals uniquely per instance (two TL071s, two sets

  Interface

  ┌───────────┬────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Hier. pin │  Type  │              Connects (main sheet)               │

  │ IN        │ Input  │ DAC VOUT (buffered 0–5V)                         │
  ├───────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ OUT       │ Output │ → filter (signal) / → output-amp offset (offset) │
  └───────────┴────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Power via global symbols inside (no pins): +15V, −15V, −8V (for the 32k
  centering current), GND.

  Inside the sub-sheet (the repeated block)

  ┌───────────┬────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │ Component │     Value      │       Role        │
  ├───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ R_in      │ 10k            │ DAC input         │
  ├───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ R_cen     │ 32k (from −8V) │ centering current │
  ├───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ R_fb      │ 10k            │ feedback (unity)  │
  ├───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ C_comp    │ 47pF           │ stability         │
  ├───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ C_dec ×2  │ 0.1µF          │ ±15V decoupling   │
  ├───────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ U (TL071) │ —              │ inverting summer  │
  └───────────┴────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  TL071 (per convention): Supply ±15V (pin 7 = +15V, pin 4 = −15V). Pins: 2 = − 
  (Input), 3 = + → GND (Input), 6 = out (Output), 1/5/8 = NC. Lives inside the
  Centering sub-sheet.

  Optional — go one step further?

  Since both the DAC and the centering follow the exact same signal/offset
  split, you could combine them into one reused "bipolar DAC source" sub-sheet
  (DAC + buffer + centering inside → outputs ±2.5V directly). That's fewer 
  top-level blocks (one per path instead of two).

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │               Approach               │               Pros               │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Separate (DAC sheet + Centering      │ more modular, digital vs analog  │
  │ sheet) ← your plan                   │ kept distinct, clearer           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Combined (DAC+buffer+centering in    │ fewer instances on the main      │
  │ one)                                 │ sheet                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  Both are valid — your instinct (separate Centering sub-sheet) is the cleaner, 
  more modular choice. Combine only if you want fewer top-level boxes.

  Verdict

  Yes — make Centering a reused sub-sheet (IN/OUT pins, power via globals incl.
  −8V), two instances CenterSignal/CenterOffset, same file. Exactly the DAC
  pattern. Good modular thinking.

✻ Cogitated for 4m 0s

❯ wait its OFSET gain and the OUTPUT AMP so they arw repeated also              

● Important clarification — in the final (recalc) design, the offset does NOT 
  have its own gain bank or post-amp. That symmetry is from the old topology.
  The recalc deliberately broke it. Here's why:

  Signal and offset are NOT symmetric (on purpose)

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             │   Needs     │                                            │
  │             │ analog gain │                    Why                     │
  │             │    bank?    │                                            │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Amplitude   │ YES —       │ scaling the waveform digitally below ~50%  │
  │ (signal)    │ CD4066      │ coarsens its shape, so you need analog     │
  │             │ octaves     │ ranges to keep the DAC ≥50%                │
  ├─────────────┼─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │             │ offset is a DC level (no shape), so the    │
  │ Offset      │ NO — fixed  │ 16-bit DAC sets it digitally (0.19mV       │
  │             │ R_off       │ steps) — lossless, no analog ranging       │
  │             │             │ needed                                     │
  └─────────────┴─────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So they look alike but have different needs. The offset's "gain bank +
  post-amp" is redundant — the digital offset DAC already does what it would.

  The recalc structure: ONE output amp, not two repeated stages

  Signal → gain bank (CD4066, variable Rg) ─┐
                                            ├─► ONE OUTPUT AMP (Rf=51k) → VOUT
  Offset → fixed R_off (10k) ───────────────┘

  Vout = −(Rf/Rg)·Vsignal − (Rf/R_off)·Voffset — one inverting summer, signal on
  a variable-gain input, offset on a fixed input. No offset gain bank, no 
  offset post-amp, no separate summing amp.

  So what to do with the repeated offset stage

  - The offset gain bank (CD4066) + offset post-amp → remove them. They're the
  old symmetric topology that the recalc eliminated.
  - You're left with one output amp (signal gain bank + fixed R_off → one
  op-amp). That's single-use → no reuse benefit → no sub-sheet needed (optional
  sub-sheet just for organization).

  If you really want the symmetric design anyway

  You can keep offset's own gain bank + post-amp and sub-sheet the reusable
  "gain+amp" block — but it costs an extra CD4066 + 595 + op-amp (and forces a
  separate summing amp) for a function the digital offset DAC already does for 
  free. I'd only do this if you specifically want hardware coarse-offset
  ranging. Otherwise it's parts for nothing.

  My recommendation

  Drop the offset gain bank + offset post-amp. Use the recalc structure: one
  output amp, signal via the gain bank, offset via fixed R_off (10k), offset
  fine-control digital. Then there's no repeated stage to sub-sheet — and you
  save ~2 op-amps + a CD4066 + a 595.

  Question to confirm: do you want offset to have analog coarse ranges (→ keep
  the symmetric bank, sub-sheet it), or is digital offset via the 16-bit DAC 
  enough (→ recalc: one output amp, drop the offset bank)? The recalc path is
  simpler and loses nothing — that's my call.

❯ ⚠ 2. You likely only need ONE CD4066 (4 switches), not two - you sure we only 
  need 1? then sill can we increase decrease ampitide in linear way liek lets   
  say for exampel 0.1V, 0.2V or liek (X+c)V then (2X+c)V ?                      
                                                                                
  ⚠ 1. Resistor values are still the OLD decade set - then give em updated list 
                                                                                
    ⚠ 3. CD4066 VDD → done                                                      
                                                                                
                                                                                

● You're right to push back — I was too hasty. Let me correct it: with 8
  switches you get a genuinely useful 8-octave range select, and that's better
  than 1 CD4066. Here's how it actually works:

  How fine linear steps happen (the key idea)

  Amplitude has two layers:
  - Analog octaves (CD4066, 1-of-8 select) → set the coarse range (×0.04 … ×5.1)
  - 16-bit DAC digital scaling → the fine linear steps (0.1V, 0.2V, …) within
  the selected octave

  So your 0.1V / 0.2V / (X+c), (2X+c) steps come from the digital DAC, and the
  octaves keep the DAC ≥50% scale so the waveform shape stays clean even at tiny
  amplitudes. That's exactly why 8 ranges help — they let you output small
  amplitudes without the shape going coarse.

  → Use the 8 switches as 1-of-8 octave RANGE select (one switch on at a time) —
  NOT binary 8-bit (that's the non-monotonic case). So keep both CD4066. I was

  Feedback: R25 (Rf) = 51k.

  With digital scaling 50–100% inside each octave, coverage is continuous from 
  ~±0.05V to ±12.75V — so any 0.1V step lands cleanly, and the DAC never drops
  below half-scale.

  Your "(X+c)" question

  - X, 2X, … (amplitude) → octave range + digital scale
  - +c (offset) → the 16-bit offset DAC

  So (X+c), (2X+c) = set amplitude via gain+digital, set the DC c via the offset
  DAC. Fully independent, both fine.

  One caveat on the big resistors

  Switch 7/8 use 620k / 1.3M — these add a little Johnson noise and rely on the
  TL071's low JFET bias (~30pA → negligible offset). Since those ranges output
  only ±0.1–0.2V, the absolute noise is tiny, so it's fine. If you'd rather
  avoid >330k, stop at 6 ranges (down to ±0.4V) and let the digital DAC cover
  smaller amplitudes (slightly coarser shape below ±0.4V).

❯ is there better alternative ic with direct 16 channels or either 8 channels   
  in sri lnka even a atmaga programambal chip                                   

● Yes — there's a much better fit, and you already use it: the CD4051B
  (8-channel analog mux). For your gain bank, you want 1-of-8 octave select —
  and a CD4051 is exactly a 1-of-8 analog selector with built-in 3-bit address 
  decoding. So you replace 2× CD4066 + a 595 with ONE CD4051 + 3 address lines.

  CD4051 gain bank (replaces the CD4066s)

  V1 (signal) ──┬─[10k ]─► X7        CD4051
                ├─[20k ]─► X6        A/B/C select the octave
                ├─[39k ]─► X5        X (common) ─► op-amp (−) summing node
                ├─[82k ]─► X4        INH → GND (enabled)
                ├─[160k]─► X3
                ├─[330k]─► X2
                ├─[620k]─► X1
                └─[1.3M]─► X0

  - 3 address bits (A/B/C) pick which octave resistor is in circuit → 8 ranges,
  one chip
  - Far fewer parts than 8 CD4066 switches + a 595
  - You already know this chip (it's your output mux) — same handling

  If you want 16 ranges → CD4067 / 74HC4067

  The CD4067 (74HC4067) is the 16-channel version (4 address bits). Common in SL
  (popular in Arduino projects). 16 octave/range resistors on X0–X15, 4 address
  lines. One chip for 16 ranges.

  ┌───────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────┬──────────┐
  │   Need    │       Chip        │ Address bits │ Channels │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 8 ranges  │ CD4051B           │ 3 (A/B/C)    │ 8        │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 16 ranges │ CD4067 / 74HC4067 │ 4            │ 16       │
  └───────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────┴──────────┘

  "ATmega-programmable chip?"

  The MCU itself can't be the analog switch — it can't pass an arbitrary ±2.5V
  analog signal through a GPIO as a low-Ron path. But the ATmega programs the 
  mux: it sets the 3–4 address bits in firmware to pick the octave. So you keep
  your existing ATmega and just drive the CD4051/CD4067 address lines — no 
  second MCU, no 595 even (3–4 direct GPIO if you have them spare).

  CD4051B spec (per convention) — for the gain bank

  Supply: VDD = +5V (logic), VEE = −8V (negative signal), VSS = GND.

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────┬───────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │         Pin         │   Name    │     Type      │      Connects to      │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 16                  │ VDD       │ Power         │ +5V                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 7                   │ VEE       │ Power         │ −8V                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 8                   │ VSS       │ Power         │ GND                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 11/10/9             │ A/B/C     │ Input         │ 3 address bits (MCU   │
  │                     │           │               │ GPIO or 595)          │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 6                   │ INH       │ Input         │ GND                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 13,14,15,12,1,5,2,4 │ X0–X7     │ Bidirectional │ 8 octave resistors    │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 3                   │ X         │ Bidirectional │ op-amp (−) summing    │
  │                     │ (common)  │               │ node                  │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────┴───────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  Caveat: the CD4051's Ron (~150–300Ω) sits in series with the selected Rg → a
  small gain error (≈2% on the 10k range, less on bigger). Same as CD4066 would
  have; calibrate it out in firmware. Use break-before-make (CD4051 is,
  inherently) so ranges don't momentarily short.

  Recommendation

  - Gain bank → CD4051B (8 octave ranges, 3 address bits) — replaces 2× CD4066 +
  595, fewer parts, you already use the chip.
  - Want 16 ranges → CD4067.
  - Drive the address lines straight from the ATmega (or 3 bits of a
  control 595).

  This is genuinely cleaner than the CD4066 approach for select (1-of-N) —
  CD4066 is better for independent on/off switches, but your gain bank is a
  selector, so the mux IC wins. Want the firmware snippet that maps a target
  amplitude → (CD4051 octave address + DAC scale)?

❯ sowe cna reduce pin usage                                                     

● Exactly — that's the win. Let me quantify it:

  Pin/chip comparison for the 8-range gain bank

  ┌───────────────────┬────────────┬───────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │     Approach      │  MCU pins  │       Chips       │  Internal wires   │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ 2× CD4066 + 595   │ 3 (595     │ 2× CD4066 + 595   │ 595 → 8 switch    │
  │                   │ ctrl)      │                   │ lines             │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ CD4051, direct    │ 3 (A/B/C)  │ 1× CD4051         │ 3 address lines   │
  │ GPIO              │            │                   │                   │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ CD4051, via       │ 0 extra    │ 1× CD4051 +       │ 3 bits of         │
  │ shared 595        │            │ shared 595        │ existing 595      │
  └───────────────────┴────────────┴───────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  So even at worst it's the same 3 pins — but you drop a whole 595 and a CD4066,
  and the fan-out goes from 8 wires to 3. And the address bits decode 
  internally (3→8), so you never wire individual switches.

  The really pin-efficient move: one shared control 595 → many mux addresses

  A single control 595 has 8 outputs. Muxes only need address bits:
  - Gain mux (CD4051): 3 bits
  - Output mux (filtered/raw): 1 bit
  - (any other selector): remaining bits

  So ONE 595 (3 MCU pins, or your existing control bus) drives ALL the mux 
  addresses — gain + output mux + spare. That's the minimum: a handful of MCU
  pins control every analog selection in the instrument.

  Or, since you freed up PB0/PB1/PB2/PB6, drive the 3 address bits straight from
  GPIO — then no 595 at all for the gain bank.

  Bonus: 16 ranges costs almost nothing extra

  Swap CD4051 (8-ch, 3 bits) → CD4067 (16-ch, 4 bits) = 16 octave ranges for
  just 1 more address line. One chip, 4 pins.

  Net effect

  - Fewer chips (CD4051 replaces 2× CD4066 [+ maybe a 595])
  - Fewer wires (3 address vs 8 switch lines — internal decode)
  - Fewer/zero extra MCU pins (direct GPIO, or share one control 595 across all
  muxes)

  So yes — moving selection from CD4066 banks to CD4051/CD4067 muxes reduces 
  pins, chips, and wiring all at once, because the mux does the 3→8 (or 4→16)
  decode for you. Your gain bank is a selector, so the mux is the right tool.

  Want me to lay out the final control map — which mux addresses come from GPIO
  vs a shared 595 — so you can see the minimal pin set for the whole
  analog-select system?

❯ no its actual componation 2^8 thats why i ten to use 8                        

● Ah — you mean 2⁸ = 256 levels by combining 8 binary-weighted switches
  (multiple ON at once), not 1-of-8 select. That changes things, and I have to
  be straight with you: that approach is non-monotonic and won't work cleanly.
  Two reasons:

  1. Binary-weighted 8-bit switched resistors = non-monotonic ❌

  For 256 correct steps, the MSB resistor must be matched to better than ½ LSB =
  1/512 ≈ 0.2%. With 1% (let alone 5%) resistors, at the big transitions the
  output goes the wrong way:

  ▎ Example: MSB resistor +1%, lower bits −1% → code 0x80 (128) produces less 
  ▎ output than 0x7F (127). Increasing the code decreases the voltage.

  That's non-monotonic, and calibration cannot fix it — a LUT can only remap a
  curve that always rises; it can't un-fold one that goes backward. So your 256
  "combinations" wouldn't be 256 clean steps — some would be out of order.

  2. CD4051 can't even do binary combos

  A CD4051 is 1-of-8 select (one channel at a time) — it physically can't sum
  multiple resistors. So binary combinations need CD4066 (independent switches)
  — which brings back the non-monotonic problem above.

  You already have 256+ MONOTONIC steps — in the 16-bit DAC

  Here's the key: your signal DAC is 16-bit. Scaling the wavetable digitally
  gives you 65,536 monotonic amplitude levels for free — far more than 256,
  perfectly monotonic, zero extra hardware:

  amplitude = (analog octave gain) × (digital scale, 16-bit) × 2.5V

  - Want 256 steps? Use 256 of the DAC's 65536 — clean and ordered.
  - Want finer? You have 65536.

  The analog bank then only needs to set the coarse octave (to keep the DAC ≥50%
  scale for shape) — that's a 1-of-N select → CD4051, 3 pins. The fine 256 (or
  more) steps are digital.

  So the right split

  ┌──────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │         Want         │          Use          │         Result          │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 256+ fine amplitude  │ 16-bit DAC, digital   │ monotonic, free, 65536  │
  │ steps                │ scaling               │ levels                  │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ coarse octave range  │ CD4051 (1-of-8        │ keeps DAC ≥50% scale    │
  │                      │ select)               │                         │
  └──────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  If you insist on analog 256 steps (full DAC always)

  The only monotonic analog way is an 8-bit R-2R multiplying DAC (R and 2R only
  — inherently monotonic, signal as the reference). You know R-2R well, so you
  could build one. But it's a whole extra ladder + switches, when your existing 
  16-bit DAC already does 256× better, monotonically, in firmware.

  Bottom line

  - 8 binary analog switches for 256 amplitude levels → non-monotonic, don't.
  - Get the 256 (really 65536) steps from the 16-bit DAC digitally — monotonic,
  free.
  - Analog bank = coarse octave select only (CD4051, 1-of-8, 3 pins).

  So you don't need 8 switches for resolution — the resolution is already in
  your DAC. The switches are just for picking the octave. Want me to show the
  firmware: target amplitude → (octave select + 16-bit scale code)?

❯ 256 levels

● I hear you on wanting 256 levels — but 256 levels does NOT require binary 
  CD4066, and binary CD4066 actually can't give you 256 clean levels. Let me be
  precise about why, and the right way:

  The trap: binary-weighted CD4066 ≠ 256 monotonic levels

  8 switches with binary-weighted resistors (R, R/2, R/4 … R/128) summed
  together = 256 combinations, but they are NOT monotonic — the MSB needs 0.2%
  matching, and with real resistors code 128 can output less than code 127. So
  you get 256 codes but they're out of order → unusable as a clean amplitude
  control, and calibration can't fix it.

  256 monotonic levels need R-2R topology, not binary

  There are only two correct ways to get 256 monotonic levels:

  ① Digital scaling of your existing 16-bit DAC (best — no CD4066)
  Your signal DAC is already a 16-bit R-2R DAC = 65,536 monotonic levels. Just
  multiply the wavetable by your amplitude factor in firmware → 256 (or 65536)
  clean, ordered levels, free, zero extra parts. This is the simplest path to
  256 levels, period.

  ② An 8-bit R-2R Multiplying DAC (if you insist on analog + CD4066)
  If you want the 256 levels done in analog (full DAC always), build an 8-bit 
  R-2R MDAC — only two resistor values (R=10k, 2R=20k), with the CD4066 switches
  steering each ladder leg (signal vs ground). R
  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ thas why isgn the cd4066                                                      
  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ wit you means instead of thsi coml9x 2 ic sapprach we can simply use 2 shift  
  register for voltage levels also how [Image #83]?                             
  ⎿  [Image #83]

❯ pleas etelel me now                                                           

● Yes — exactly! You've got it. The two shift registers (your 16-bit DAC) make
  the waveform and set the amplitude — digitally. That eliminates the entire 
  CD4066 gain bank. Here's the how:

  How it works — scale the wavetable in firmware

  The DAC value = waveform sample scaled around the midpoint (0x8000 = 0V
  output):

  // amplitude_level: 0..256  (gives 256 monotonic steps; use 0..65535 for 
  finer)
  int32_t centered = (int32_t)wavetable[i] - 32768;        // ±32767 about 
  center
  int32_t scaled   = centered * amplitude_level / 256;     // scale amplitude
  uint16_t dac_val = 32768 + scaled;                        // re-center
  dac16_write(dac_val);

  - amplitude_level = 256 → full ±2.5V
  - = 128 → ±1.25V
  - = 1 → tiny
  - 256 monotonic steps (it's just multiplication — always ordered, never folds
  backward like binary switches would). Want more? Use a 16-bit factor → 65,536
  steps.

  Because you scale around 0x8000, the signal stays centered — the 32k centering
  still works.

  What changes in hardware

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │ Old (CD4066 gain bank) │ New (digital amplitude) │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 2× CD4066 (U17, U23)   │ removed ✅              │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ gain-control 595 (U26) │ removed (frees pins) ✅ │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ variable-gain post-amp │ fixed-gain post-amp     │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  The post-amp U20 becomes a fixed gain ×5.1 (Rf = 51k, Rin = 10k) — just enough
  to bring full-scale ±2.5V up to ±12.75V. Amplitude below that is all digital.

  DAC (scaled) → center → ×5.1 fixed post-amp → ±12.75V max
                            (amplitude set digitally, 256+ levels)

  The one caveat (and the fix if you care)

  At very low amplitude (e.g. level 1–10 of 256), the DAC uses few codes → the
  shape gets coarse (8-bit at the very bottom). At medium/high amplitude it's
  full 16-bit.

  - For most use this is fine — skip the CD4066 entirely.
  - If you want clean shape even at tiny amplitudes, add a few coarse octave 
  ranges with ONE CD4051 (1-of-4 or 1-of-8 select, 3 pins) to keep the DAC ≥50%
  scale. Far simpler than the CD4066 binary bank, and monotonic.

  Bottom line

  - Remove the 2× CD4066 gain bank + its 595. ✅
  - 256 (or 65536) monotonic amplitude levels come from scaling the 16-bit DAC 
  in firmware — the two shift registers do it.
  - Post-amp = fixed ×5.1 (Rf 51k / Rin 10k) for the ±13V max.
  - Optional: one CD4051 for a couple of coarse octaves if you want perfect
  shape at very low amplitude.

  This is the simplest, most monotonic, fewest-parts way to get your 256 levels
  — and you already have the hardware (the DAC). The CD4066 binary bank was the
  hard, broken way; digital scaling is the clean way.

● Here's the simplified signal output stage — no CD4066 gain bank, amplitude
  done digitally in the DAC, one fixed-gain post-amp.

  Full chain (block flow)

  [16-bit Signal DAC] → [Buffer] → [U7 center ±2.5V] → [Sallen-Key] → [CD4051
  mux] → [POST-AMP ×5.1 + offset] → [Protection] → BNC
        ▲ amplitude scaled
           ▲ offset (also ×5.1)
          digitally (256+ levels)
  [Offset DAC]→[U10 center]→ R_off

  The post-amp / output stage (the part that changed)

  Fixed-gain inverting summer — signal and offset both enter, gain ×5.1, no
  switches:

   SELSIG (±2.5V from mux,                         Rf 51k
    amplitude already set in DAC)          ┌───[====]───┐
          │                                │   (22pF ∥) │
         [Rin 10k]──────────────●──────────┤            │
                                │ (− input)│            │
   OFFSET (±2.5V) ──[R_off 10k]─┤          │  U20 TL071 │
    (from offset DAC + U10)     │          └──┤−        │
                           GND ─┼─────────────┤+        ●───► VOUT (±12.75V) →
  protection
                                │             └─────────┘
                            ±15V supply + 0.1µF decoupling

  Vout = −(51k/10k)·SELSIG − (51k/10k)·OFFSET = −5.1·(signal + offset)

  - Signal amplitude → set by digital scaling in the DAC (256+ monotonic
  levels), not by changing Rin
  - Offset → set by the 16-bit offset DAC (digital), enters via fixed R_off
  - Gain is fixed ×5.1 → full-scale = ±12.75V; smaller amplitudes come from the
  DAC

  POST-AMP — TL071 (per convention)

  Supply: ±15V (pin 7 = +15V, pin 4 = −15V) + 0.1µF decoupling each rail.

  ┌─────┬────────┬────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Pin │  Name  │  Type  │           Connects to           │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2   │ − (in) │ Input  │ summing node (Rin + R_off + Rf) │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3   │ + (in) │ Input  │ GND                             │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 6   │ out    │ Output │ VOUT → protection               │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 7   │ V+     │ Power  │ +15V                            │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 4   │ V−     │ Power  │ −15V                            │
  └─────┴────────┴────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  Components: Rin = 10k, R_off = 10k, Rf = 51k, C = 22pF (across Rf, stability).

  What's REMOVED vs the old design

  - ❌ 2× CD4066 gain bank (U17, U23)
  - ❌ gain-control 595 (U26) — frees its pins
  - ❌ 8 gain resistors (5.6k/56k/560k/5.6M…)
  - ✅ Replaced by: one fixed-gain post-amp + digital amplitude scaling in the 
  DAC

  Rest of the chain (unchanged, for reference)

  - Buffer + U7 centering (10k in, 32k from −8V, 10k fb) → ±2.5V centered
  - Sallen-Key filter (smooth) + CD4051 mux (smooth/raw select, VDD=+5V/VEE=−8V)
  - Protection (dual-rail BC557/BC547 + LM393, R_out, clamps)

  CD4051 (1-of-4 octave select, 3 pins) before the post-amp to keep the DAC ≥50%
  scale. Otherwise this is the whole thing — far simpler than the CD4066 bank.

❯ is it simply the R-2R ladder gain                                             

❯ what abotu QA(MSB)        QB             QC    ...   QP(LSB)                  
   |              |              |           |                                  
  20k            20k            20k         20k                                 
   |              |              |           |                                  
   +---10k---+---10k---+---10k--+--- ... ---+--- Vout                           
             |         |                                                        
            20k       20k                                                       
             |         |                                                        
            GND       GND                                                       

● Two things to untangle here — the concept and your ladder drawing.

  It's not the ladder's "gain" — it's the DAC code

  The R-2R ladder is a fixed linear DAC:
  $$V_\text{out} = \frac{\text{code}}{2^{16}} \times V_\text{ref}$$

  The ladder's transfer never changes — it always maps code → voltage linearly.
  Amplitude control = changing the code you send (scaling the wavetable in
  firmware), not changing any "gain" in the ladder.

  amplitude = (digital code, set in firmware) × (DAC step) × (fixed post-amp
  ×5.1)
                ↑ this is what you vary            ↑ fixed ladder    ↑ fixed
  analog gain

  So: scale the wavetable digitally → the code shrinks/grows → Vout
  shrinks/grows linearly → 256+ monotonic amplitude levels. The fixed ×5.1 
  post-amp just lifts the DAC's ±2.5V to ±12.75V. No "ladder gain" knob exists.

  Your ladder drawing has an error — fix it

  You drew 20k-to-GND at internal nodes. That's wrong — it corrupts the binary
  weighting. A correct R-2R has only ONE 20k termination, at the LSB end:

   QA(MSB)─[20k]─nA─[10k]─nB─[10k]─nC─ ... ─nP─[10k]─(LSB node)
                  │        │        │              │
              (Vout)    [20k]    [20k]          [20k]      [20k]
                         QB        QC            QP      termination
                                                            │
                                                           GND

  The rule:
  - each bit → one 20k (2R) → its node ✓
  - nodes chained by 10k (R) series ✓
  - exactly ONE 20k (2R) termination → GND, at the far (LSB) end ✓
  - Vout at the MSB end ✓
  - ❌ NO 20k-to-GND on the middle nodes (that's the mistake in your sketch)

  Good news: the ladder you actually built earlier is correct — we verified it
  (R10 = single termination at the LSB end, 20k shunts per bit, 10k spine, and
  the ramp test came out monotonic). So your hardware is right; just the sketch
  above has the extra GND shunts.

  Why this gives clean amplitude

  Because Vout ∝ code (linear, monotonic), scaling the code in firmware scales
  the amplitude proportionally and monotonically — exactly what binary CD4066
  switches couldn't do. The ladder does the heavy lifting; you just pick the
  code.

  So, concretely

  - Amplitude = digital code (firmware scales the wavetable) → 256+ monotonic
  levels ✓
  - Ladder = fixed linear code→voltage converter (your build is correct: one
  termination, no middle GND shunts)
  - Post-amp = fixed ×5.1 to reach ±12.75V
  - No gain bank, no CD4066 for amplitude

  Want me to show the exact firmware line that turns "amplitude = level 0–255"
  into the scaled 16-bit DAC code?

● Okay — you've decided on 2× CD4066, binary-weighted, 256 steps. Here are the
  values. I'll give them straight, then the one caveat you need to know so
  you're not surprised on the bench.

  8-bit binary-weighted resistor values

  Inverting summer, Rf = 51k, MSB resistor = 20k, each bit doubles:

  ┌──────────┬────────┬───────────┬──────┬──────────────┐
  │   Bit    │ Weight │ R (ideal) │ E24  │ E96 (better) │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ b7 (MSB) │ ×128   │ 20.0k     │ 20k  │ 20.0k        │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ b6       │ ×64    │ 40.0k     │ 39k  │ 40.2k        │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ b5       │ ×32    │ 80.0k     │ 82k  │ 80.6k        │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ b4       │ ×16    │ 160k      │ 160k │ 160k         │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ b3       │ ×8     │ 320k      │ 330k │ 316k         │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ b2       │ ×4     │ 640k      │ 620k │ 649k         │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ b1       │ ×2     │ 1.28M     │ 1.3M │ 1.28M        │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┼──────┼──────────────┤
  │ b0 (LSB) │ ×1     │ 2.56M     │ 2.4M │ 2.55M        │
  └──────────┴────────┴───────────┴──────┴──────────────┘

  Feedback: Rf = 51k → max gain (all bits on) ≈ ×5.1 → ±12.75V at full code.
  Code n (0–255) gives gain ≈ (n/255)·5.1.

  Use E96 (1%) — E24 (5%) will be badly out of order.

  The caveat (so you're not surprised)

  At the major-bit transitions (codes 64, 128, 192) the output can step backward
  with real resistors — because the one big resistor (e.g. b7=20k) replaces the
  sum of all smaller ones, and tolerances don't line up. With 1% parts, code 
  128 can output less than code 127. A calibration LUT can't fix this (it can't
  unfold a backward step). Also, 2.4M / 1.3M are noisy and TL071-bias sensitive
  (though bias is tiny).

  So you'll get 256 codes, but they won't be 256 perfectly-ordered steps —
  expect small glitches at 64/128/192. For a campus demo that's usually
  acceptable; just know that's the source if you see it.

  If you want it monotonic later (no rework now)

  - Easiest: scale the 16-bit DAC digitally → 256 (really 65536) monotonic
  steps, zero extra chips (you already have the DAC). This is genuinely the
  better path.
  - All-analog monotonic: an R-2R MDAC — but that needs SPDT per bit = 4× CD4066
  (16 switches), only 10k/20k resistors.

  Rest of the stage (the "other I asked")

  - Post-amp / summing op-amp: TL071, ±15V, Rf = 51k, 22pF across Rf, 0.1µF
  decoupling each rail
  - Offset enters the same summing node via fixed R_off = 10k (offset level from
  the 16-bit offset DAC)
  - CD4066 VDD = +5V, VSS = −8V (logic-level fix)
  - Control: the binary bits come from a 595 (8 outputs → 8 switch controls)

  So: values above (E96), Rf = 51k, CD4066 at +5V/−8V — that's your binary
  256-step bank. Want the firmware that writes an 8-bit amplitude code to the
  gain-bank 595 (and how to pair it with the offset DAC)?

  Three things to check / be aware of

  1. Firmware bit-mapping (verify): make sure the 595 bit you call MSB drives 
  the 20k switch (largest weight) and LSB drives 2.56M. From the layout
  R21=2.56M is top, R34=20k is bottom — confirm QA…QH → switches map so the
  code's weighting is right, or the output will be scrambled.

  2. CD4066 Ron adds error (the accepted-tradeoff detail): the switch's
  ~150–200Ω Ron is in series with each resistor.
  - On the 20k MSB: 200Ω = 1% error → worsens the monotonicity at the 128
  transition
  - On big resistors: negligible
  - Also, Ron varies with signal voltage → a little distortion as the ±2.5V
  swings. Minor, but it's there.

  3. Confirm IND = the centered signal (the mux output / ±2.5V from U7), not the
  raw 0–5V. The gain bank should sit after centering.

  The accepted caveats (just so they're on record)

  - Non-monotonic at codes 64/128/192 with real resistors (inherent to binary
  8-bit; calibration can't fix the fold)
  - 2.56M / 1.28M are noisy + bias-sensitive (TL071's JFET bias is tiny, so OK)
  - Ron modulation adds slight distortion

  These are the known costs of the binary-CD4066 route you chose — fine for a
  campus build, just don't be surprised by small glitches at the major-bit
  codes.

  Verdict

  Good — the binary gain bank is wired correctly (values, Rf, +5V/−8V, switches
  → summing → U20). Confirm the bit-mapping and that IND is the centered signal,
  and it'll work (with the expected non-monotonic glitches at major bits).

❯ so can it be act as deamp and amp right?                                      

❯ explain what si goinfg ot be components insde this yellow cored "OFFSET GAIN" 
  and "OUTPUT AMP" [Image #88]?                                                 
  ⎿  [Image #88]

● Yes — one gain bank does BOTH de-amp and amp. With Rf=51k and the binary
  resistors (20k…2.56M), the gain spans:

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────┬───────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │      Code       │ Effective Rin │ Gain  │           Mode            │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┼───────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ low (LSB only)  │ ~2.56M        │ ×0.02 │ strong de-amp (attenuate) │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┼───────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ ~code 50        │ ~51k          │ ×1.0  │ unity                     │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┼───────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ high (all bits) │ ~10k          │ ×5.1  │ amplify                   │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────┴───────┴───────────────────────────┘

  So as the 8-bit code rises, gain goes from ×0.02 → ×5.1 — it attenuates small
  signals and amplifies large ones with the same bank. ✓

  What's inside "OUTPUT AMP" (the yellow block)

  It's the signal gain bank + the summing op-amp, all in one stage:

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Component       │                     What                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8 binary resistors   │ 20k…2.56M (the ones you just placed)         │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2× CD4066 (U17, U23) │ the 8-bit switches (+5V/−8V)                 │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ U20 — TL071 op-amp   │ inverting summer, Rf = 51k, 22pF across Rf   │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ R_off (offset input) │ brings the offset into the same summing node │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Vout = −(Rf/Rin_code)·signal − (Rf/R_off)·offset → does gain (de-amp/amp) AND 
  offset sum in one op-amp, output ±13V.

  TL071 (per convention): ±15V supply (pin 7=+15, pin 4=−15), pin 2=− (summing),
  pin 3=+ (GND), pin 6=out. 0.1µF decoupling each rail.

  What's inside "OFFSET GAIN" (U21) — and my recommendation

  In the old symmetric design this block was another full gain bank (8 binary
  resistors + 2 CD4066 + post-amp + 595) scaling the offset.

  But offset doesn't need a gain bank — it's a DC level, set digitally by the
  16-bit offset DAC. To reach ±13V it only needs a fixed ×5.1:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Recommended "OFFSET GAIN"  │                                            │
  │          contents          │                                            │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Just R_off = 10k           │ offset enters the OUTPUT AMP's summing     │
  │                            │ node at fixed ×5.1                         │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Offset level               │ set by the 16-bit offset DAC (digital,     │
  │                            │ fine)                                      │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  → Drop the offset CD4066 bank entirely. Replace the whole "OFFSET GAIN" block
  with a single R_off = 10k resistor. The 16-bit offset DAC already gives you
  fine, monotonic offset over the full ±13V — a second binary bank would be
  redundant (and non-monotonic).

  So, concretely

  SIGNAL → [8-bit gain bank: 8 binary R + 2 CD4066] ─┐
                                                     ├─► U20 (TL071, Rf=51k) →
  ±13V
  OFFSET (16-bit DAC, centered ±2.5V) → [R_off 10k] ─┘

  - OUTPUT AMP = signal gain bank + U20 summing op-amp (de-amp/amp + offset) ✓
  - OFFSET GAIN = should be just R_off = 10k (not a CD4066 bank) — offset is
  digital

  Why offset ≠ amplitude (the reason for the asymmetry)

  - Amplitude scales the waveform → wants the analog gain bank (de-amp/amp)
  - Offset is a DC level → digital DAC sets it; only needs fixed gain to reach
  ±13V

  So your signal gain bank does deamp+amp (good), but don't duplicate it for 
  offset — use a fixed R_off + the offset DAC. Want me to show how the offset
  DAC value maps to a target offset voltage through the fixed ×5.1?

❯ yes for : 1. Firmware bit-mapping (verify): make sure the 595 bit you call    
  MSB drives the 20k switch (largest weight) and LSB drives

● Here's the definitive mapping. The trick: in a 74HC595 with MSB-first
  shifting, QA ends up as the byte's LSB and QH as the MSB. So wire QA→smallest
  weight, QH→largest weight, and you can send the code directly.

  The mapping (wire QA→QH sequentially to the resistors in weight order)

  ┌───────────┬────────┬────────────┬─────────────────────────┬────────────┐
  │ Code bit  │ Weight │  Resistor  │  CD4066 section (ctrl   │  Wire to   │
  │           │        │            │          pin)           │    595     │
  ├───────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ bit0      │ ×1     │ 2.56M      │ U17A (pin 13)           │ QA         │
  │ (LSB)     │        │ (R21)      │                         │            │
  ├───────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ bit1      │ ×2     │ 1.28M      │ U17B (pin 5)            │ QB         │
  │           │        │ (R22)      │                         │            │
  ├───────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ bit2      │ ×4     │ 640k (R23) │ U17C (pin 6)            │ QC         │
  ├───────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ bit3      │ ×8     │ 320k (R24) │ U17D (pin 12)           │ QD         │
  ├───────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ bit4      │ ×16    │ 160k (R31) │ U23A (pin 13)           │ QE         │
  ├───────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ bit5      │ ×32    │ 80k (R32)  │ U23B (pin 5)            │ QF         │
  ├───────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ bit6      │ ×64    │ 40k (R33)  │ U23C (pin 6)            │ QG         │
  ├───────────┼────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ bit7      │ ×128   │ 20k (R34)  │ U23D (pin 12)           │ QH         │
  │ (MSB)     │        │            │                         │            │
  └───────────┴────────┴────────────┴─────────────────────────┴────────────┘

  Convenient: your resistors are already laid out in weight order (R21=2.56M …
  R34=20k), so just wire QA→U17A, QB→U17B, QC→U17C, QD→U17D, QE→U23A, QF→U23B, 
  QG→U23C, QH→U23D — straight across. Then sending the byte directly gives
  correct weighting.

  Why QA=LSB (the 595 shift rule)

  On each SRCLK, SER→QA, QA→QB, … So after 8 MSB-first clocks: QA = bit0, QB = 
  bit1, … QH = bit7. That's why QA must drive the lowest weight (2.56M) and QH
  the highest (20k).

  Firmware

  // gain_set: write 8-bit amplitude code to the gain-bank 595 (MSB-first)
  void gain_set(uint8_t code) {
      // SER/SRCLK on the control bus; here shown for the gain latch
      spi_or_bitbang_send_msb_first(code);   // MSB-first → QA=bit0 … QH=bit7
      GAIN_LATCH_HIGH;                        // PB-spare latch pulse
      GAIN_LATCH_LOW;
  }

  Amplitude ↔ code (Rf=51k, DAC full-scale ±2.5V):
  $$\text{gain}(n) = \frac{51\text{k}}{2.56\text{M}},n \approx 0.02,n, \qquad
  \hat V_\text{out} \approx 0.05,n ;\text{V}$$

  So: code = round(target_Vpeak / 0.05) → e.g. 1V → code 20, 0.1V → code 2,
  12.75V → code 255.

  Verification test (write these, measure DOUT × the post-amp)

  ┌──────┬────────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────┐
  │ Code │    Selected    │ Expected gain │ ~Output peak │
  ├──────┼────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 0    │ none           │ 0             │ 0V           │
  ├──────┼────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 1    │ 2.56M (LSB)    │ ×0.02         │ ±0.05V       │
  ├──────┼────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 128  │ 20k (MSB only) │ ×2.55         │ ±6.4V        │
  ├──────┼────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 255  │ all            │ ×5.1          │ ±12.75V      │
  └──────┴────────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────┘

  Key monotonicity check: compare code 127 vs 128. Ideally 128 > 127. If 128
  comes out lower, that's the binary non-monotonicity at the MSB transition
  (resistor tolerance) — expected with this approach. Note where it happens so
  firmware can avoid those exact codes if needed.

  Summary

  - Wire QA→U17A(2.56M, LSB) … QH→U23D(20k, MSB) — straight across, weight order
  - Send the 8-bit code directly (MSB-first); amplitude ≈ 0.05 × code V
  - Verify 127 vs 128 to see the non-monotonic spot

  That's the bit-mapping locked. Want the combined routine that sets amplitude 
  (this bank) + offset (the 16-bit offset DAC) + waveform together?

✻ Baked for 3m 18s

❯ what shoul be atmag conn pins PC0 are currently free also [Image #90]         
  ⎿  [Image #90]

● Connect the GainSignal 595 to the shared control bus + its own latch — that's
  the consistent, pin-efficient choice, and it keeps your whole PC port free.

  Recommended connections

  ┌──────────────┬────────────────┬──────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ GainSignal   │      Net       │ ATmega   │            Notes             │
  │     pin      │                │   pin    │                              │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ SER          │ PB0_CTRL_SER   │ PB0      │ shared with the mux 595      │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ SRCLK        │ PB1_CTRL_SRCLK │ PB1      │ shared                       │
  ├──────────────┼────────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ RCLK         │ PB6_GAIN_RCLK  │ PB6      │ its own latch (PB6 was       │
  │              │                │          │ reserved for this)           │
  └──────────────┴────────────────┴──────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  So SER/SRCLK ride the control bus (same as the OutputMux 595), and the gain
  bank gets its own latch on PB6. This matches the pattern: all control 595s 
  share SER+SRCLK, each has a separate RCLK.

  Why not use PC0?

  You can (PC0/PC1/PC2 = SER/SRCLK/RCLK, dedicated) — but sharing the control
  bus is more efficient and leaves all 8 PC pins free for future expansion
  (extra channels, a display, more controls). Only spend PC pins if you
  specifically want this 595 fully independent.

  How the shared bus works (rare updates, so fine)

  - Update gain: shift the 8-bit amplitude byte → pulse PB6 (gain latch)
  - Update mux: shift the mux byte → pulse PB2 (mux latch)
  - Both 595s see the shifted data, but only the one whose latch you pulse
  updates its output (the other holds). Same trick as your DAC sharing. Since
  gain/mux change rarely, no timing concern.

  (If you'd rather, daisy-chain the control 595s — QH′ of the mux 595 → SER of 
  the gain 595 — then one shift loads all control bits and you latch each. 
  Either works; shared-bus is simpler to wire across sub-sheets.)

  Final control-pin map

  ┌─────────┬────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │   Pin   │    Net     │          Drives           │
  ├─────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PB0     │ CTRL_SER   │ all control 595s (shared) │
  ├─────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PB1     │ CTRL_SRCLK │ all control 595s (shared) │
  ├─────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PB2     │ MUX_RCLK   │ mux 595 latch             │
  ├─────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PB6     │ GAIN_RCLK  │ gain 595 latch            │
  ├─────────┼────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ PC0–PC7 │ free       │ future use                │
  └─────────┴────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  So: SER←PB0, SRCLK←PB1 (shared bus), RCLK←PB6 for GainSignal. PC port stays
  open. Want me to update the pin map in the docs to include PB6_GAIN_RCLK?

● No, don't sub-sheet the summing amp — you're right. It's a single op-amp + 3
  resistors, used once. Sub-sheets earn their keep for reused blocks (DAC,
  Centering, gain_stage) or large blocks (filter). A 1-op-amp unity summer is
  neither → keep it on the main sheet. Sub-sheeting it would just add
  click-through for no benefit.

  Summing amp (keep on main sheet) — per convention:
  - TL071, ±15V (pin7=+15, pin4=−15), 0.1µF decoupling
  - V_SIG via R20 (4.7k), V_OFFSET via R17 (4.7k), feedback R15 (4.7k) → unity
  sum
  - pin3 (+) → GND, 47pF across feedback

  GainOffset control pins (since you're building it symmetric)

  Same pattern as GainSignal — share the control bus, own latch:

  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GainOffset pin │       Net        │            ATmega pin            │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SER            │ PB0_CTRL_SER     │ PB0 (shared)                     │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SRCLK          │ PB1_CTRL_SRCLK   │ PB1 (shared)                     │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ RCLK           │ PC0_OFFGAIN_RCLK │ PC0 (new latch — PB is now full) │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  PB0–PB7 are all used now, so the offset-gain latch goes on PC0 (which you said
  is free). SER/SRCLK still ride the shared control bus.

  Updated latch map

  ┌─────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │   Pin   │         Latch         │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ PB2     │ MUX_RCLK              │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ PB6     │ GAIN_RCLK (signal)    │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ PC0     │ OFFGAIN_RCLK (offset) │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ PB3/PB4 │ signal/offset DAC     │
  └─────────┴───────────────────────┘

  One-line reminder (not pushing, just on record)

  You've gone symmetric (offset has its own gain bank + post-amp + summing amp =
  3 op-amps). That works — just know the offset gain bank is optional: a fixed
  R_off + the 16-bit offset DAC would do the same with fewer parts. But if you
  want offset to deamp/amp like the signal, your symmetric build is the way, and
  the wiring above is correct.

  So: summing amp → main sheet (no sub-sheet), GainOffset → SER/SRCLK shared 
  (PB0/PB1), RCLK = PC0.

✻ Worked for 2m 48s

❯ [Image #92] i alo though that offsetgain is not that much use. what do you    
  say can we still make offset ampitide go higher with only                     
  DAC16_R2R_DDS_OFFSET for full rnage of offset?                                
  ⎿  [Image #92]

● Yes — absolutely. Drop GainOffset. The 16-bit offset DAC alone gives you the
  full ±13V offset range with fine resolution — you just need a fixed ×5.1 gain 
  on the offset input of the summing amp. Here's how:

  Offset path without GainOffset

  Offset DAC (16-bit, 0–5V) → CenterOffset (±2.5V) → [R_off=10k into summing amp
  ×5.1] → ±12.75V offset

  The ×5.1 comes from the summing amp, not a gain bank:
  - V_offset_contribution = −(Rf/R_off)·(±2.5V) = −(51k/10k)·2.5 = ±12.75V ✓
  full range

  Why this works (and is actually better)

  - Range: ±12.75V — same as the signal ✓
  - Resolution: 16-bit over ±12.75V = 25.5V / 65536 ≈ 0.39 mV per step — far
  finer than any analog bank
  - Monotonic (it's the R-2R DAC) — no glitches, unlike the binary gain bank
  - Offset is a DC level, so digital scaling has no shape penalty (the reason it
  doesn't need analog ranging like amplitude does)

  Summing amp values (the only change)

  The summing amp gives unity to the signal (already amplified by GainSignal)
  and ×5.1 to the offset:

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────┬──────┬───────────────────────┐
  │            Input            │  Resistor  │ Gain │          Why          │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ V_SIG (from GainSignal)     │ R_sig =    │ ×1   │ signal already at     │
  │                             │ 51k        │      │ ±13V                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ V_OFFSET (from              │ R_off =    │ ×5.1 │ lifts ±2.5V → ±12.75V │
  │ CenterOffset)               │ 10k        │      │                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────┼──────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ feedback                    │ Rf = 51k   │ —    │                       │
  └─────────────────────────────┴────────────┴──────┴───────────────────────┘

  Vout = −(V_SIG) − 5.1·(V_OFFSET) — both reach full ±13V.

  What you save by dropping GainOffset

  - ❌ 2× CD4066 (offset)
  - ❌ offset gain 595
  - ❌ offset post-amp op-amp
  - ❌ PC0 latch pin freed (no OFFGAIN_RCLK needed)

  Firmware

  Offset level = set by the 16-bit offset DAC code:
  $$V_\text{offset} = 5.1 \times \big(\text{DAC}\text{off}/65536 \times 5 - 
  2.5\big), \quad \text{code} = 65536 \times \left(\frac{V\text{target}/5.1 +
  2.5}{5}\right)$$

  Rail rule (firmware): |offset| + amplitude ≤ 12.75V.

  So your instinct is right

  - Keep: DAC16_R2R_DDS_OFFSET + CenterOffset
  - Drop: GainOffset (the whole sub-sheet instance)
  - Change: summing amp's offset input resistor → R_off = 10k (gives the ×5.1),
  signal input = 51k, Rf = 51k

  Full offset range, finer resolution, fewer parts, monotonic, and PC0 stays
  free. The offset gain bank really was redundant — the DAC does it better. Want
  the combined firmware (amplitude code + offset code + waveform → all the
  registers/DACs)?

✻ Cooked for 2m 25s

❯ wait then ofset is always ampfed *5.1 then wheter is the smooth control?      

● Good catch — but the ×5.1 is fixed, and the smooth control is the 16-bit DAC 
  code, not the gain. They're two different things:

  The ×5.1 sets the range; the DAC code sets the fine position

  - ×5.1 = fixed "full-scale" mapping → lets the offset reach ±12.75V
  - 16-bit DAC code = the smooth knob → picks where in that range you sit

  You don't change the gain to adjust offset — you change the DAC code. The DAC
  has 65,536 levels, so:

  $$\text{step} = \underbrace{\frac{5\text V}{65536}}_{76,\mu\text V \text{ at
  DAC}} \times 5.1 = \mathbf{0.39\ mV} \text{ per code at the output}$$

  → 65,536 smooth offset levels spread over ±12.75V. That's very fine.

  Example — vary the code, get smooth offset

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │ Offset DAC code │ Output offset │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 0x8000 (mid)    │ 0 V           │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 0x8001          │ +0.39 mV      │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 0x8002          │ +0.78 mV      │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 0xC000          │ +6.4 V        │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 0xFFFF          │ +12.75 V      │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 0x0000          │ −12.75 V      │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────┘

  So the offset glides smoothly from −12.75V to +12.75V in 0.39mV steps — just
  by stepping the DAC code. The fixed ×5.1 never limits smoothness; it only sets
  the span.

  Analogy

  ×5.1 is the zoom level (full screen = ±12.75V). The 16-bit DAC code is the 
  cursor position on that screen — 65,536 positions = silky-smooth.

  The nice irony

  Your offset is actually SMOOTHER than your amplitude:
  - Offset = 16-bit DAC × fixed gain → 65,536 monotonic steps, 0.39mV ✓
  - Amplitude = binary CD4066 gain bank → 256 steps, non-monotonic at major bits

  So fixing the offset gain at ×5.1 loses nothing — the DAC resolution gives you
  the smooth control, and it's finer than the gain-bank amplitude. The offset
  gain bank really was pointless.

  Bottom line: smooth offset control = stepping the 16-bit offset DAC code
  (0.39mV resolution), with the ×5.1 just setting the ±12.75V span. No knob, no
  bank — the DAC is the smooth control.

✻ Cogitated for 2m 27s

❯ the PB0 and PB1 pin sharign is okay since thsi is singel threaded signel      
  cored process right? so only one precess at a time                            

● Right idea — single-threaded means operations are sequential (no two bus
  writes ever overlap). But the thing that actually makes shared PB0/PB1 correct
  is the latch discipline, not just single-threading. Let me be precise:

  Why it's safe (two reasons)

  1. Single-threaded → no collision ✓
  The ATmega does one thing at a time, so you can't be mid-shift to two 595s at
  once. Each ctrl_write() runs to completion.

  2. Latch discipline → only the target updates (this is the key rule)
  Because SER+SRCLK are shared, when you shift data it enters every 595's shift
  register. But outputs only change when you pulse that chip's RCLK. So:
  - Shift the full byte → pulse only the target's latch → target updates, others
  hold (their shift registers got the data but weren't latched)
  - Next time you update another 595, you shift its fresh byte (overwriting the
  stale data in its register) before latching it

  void ctrl_write(uint8_t data, volatile uint8_t *port, uint8_t latch) {
      for (int8_t i = 7; i >= 0; i--) {            // MSB-first on shared bus
          if (data & (1<<i)) PORTB |=  (1<<PB0);   // SER
          else               PORTB &= ~(1<<PB0);
          PORTB |=  (1<<PB1); PORTB &= ~(1<<PB1);  // SRCLK pulse
      }
      *port |=  (1<<latch);  *port &= ~(1<<latch); // pulse ONLY the target 
  latch
  }
  // ctrl_write(mux_code,  &PORTB, PB2);  // mux  — others hold
  // ctrl_write(gain_code, &PORTB, PB6);  // gain — others hold

  The DDS won't interfere

  - The 40kHz DDS uses the DAC's hardware SPI (PB5/PB7) — a different bus from
  the control bus (PB0/PB1). They don't share pins.
  - The DDS ISR doesn't touch PB0/PB1, so even if it fires mid-shift, it can't
  inject spurious clocks — your control shift just pauses and resumes (SRCLK
  only advances on your command). The byte is intact, then you latch.

  So: separate buses + sequential execution + latch discipline = sharing PB0/PB1
  across all control 595s is safe.

  One practical note

  Control updates (mux/gain) are rare (you change them when the user picks a
  range, not every sample). So even the small per-shift time is irrelevant. The
  hot path (40kHz DDS) is untouched.

  Summary

  - ✅ Single-threaded → no bus collision (your reasoning is right)
  - ✅ Plus the real correctness rule: shift full byte → latch only the target
  (others hold)
  - ✅ Control bus (PB0/PB1) is independent of the DAC SPI (PB5/PB7) → DDS never
  conflicts

  So yes — share PB0/PB1 freely across the control 595s. Just follow "shift
  fresh, latch one" in firmware and you're golden.

❯ explain mathematial yhwo thsi center wave from 2.5 to 0 [Image #94]
