● Negative offset is already there — it comes from the offset DAC going above 
  mid-scale + the inverting op-amp on ±15V. You don't need a separate "negative
  source." Let me show the math.

  The centering math (image 94)

  U10 is an inverting summer. Its (+) input is at GND, so the (−) input is a
  virtual ground (0V). Sum the currents into that node:

  $$\frac{V_\text{in}}{R99} + \frac{-8}{R100} + \frac{V_\text{out}}{R101} = 0$$

  Plug in R99=10k, R100=32k, R101=10k:

  $$\frac{V_\text{in}}{10k} + \frac{-8}{32k} + \frac{V_\text{out}}{10k} = 0$$

  Solve for V_out:

  $$V_\text{out} = -V_\text{in} + 8\cdot\frac{10k}{32k} = -V_\text{in} + 2.5 =
  \boxed{-(V_\text{in} - 2.5)}$$

  ┌────────────┬────────────────┐
  │ V_in (DAC) │     V_out      │
  ├────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 0 V        │ +2.5 V         │
  ├────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 2.5 V      │ 0 V ← centered │
  ├────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ 5 V        │ −2.5 V         │
  └────────────┴────────────────┘

  So input 0–5V (centered on 2.5) → output ±2.5V centered on 0. The 2.5V
  midpoint maps to 0V.

  Where the NEGATIVE comes from

  Look at the table: when V_in > 2.5V, V_out is negative. That's your negative
  offset. Two things make it possible:

  1. The op-amp runs on ±15V → its output can swing below ground (down to
  ~−13.5V). When the math says −2.5V, the op-amp actually pulls its output to
  −2.5V (sinks current). That is the negative source — the op-amp's −15V rail,
  not a special circuit.
  2. The −8V/32k injection just shifts the center to 0 (provides the +2.5V
  offset current). It's the reference, not the output.

  So there's no missing component — the inverting amp + ±15V supply produce
  negative automatically.

  Full bipolar offset (the chain in image 93)

  Offset DAC 0–5V → CenterOffset → ±2.5V → GainOffset (×M) → Summing Amp →
  output
                       ▲ −(Vin−2.5)         ▲ amplify         ▲ add to signal

  ┌───────────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │    Offset DAC code    │ CenterOffset out │ After ×M (e.g. 5.1) │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ code < 0x8000 (<2.5V) │ positive         │ up to +12.75V       │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ code = 0x8000 (2.5V)  │ 0                │ 0                   │
  ├───────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ code > 0x8000 (>2.5V) │ negative         │ down to −12.75V     │
  └───────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  So negative offset = set the offset DAC above mid-scale (code > 0x8000). The
  CenterOffset inverts it to negative, GainOffset amplifies, the summing amp
  adds it → the whole waveform shifts down.

  Same applies to the signal

  CenterSignal does the identical −(Vin−2.5) — that's why your signal centers on
  0 too, and can swing both polarities.

  TL;DR

  - Negative offset comes from the offset DAC going > 2.5V (code > 0x8000) →
  inverting CenterOffset outputs negative → op-amp's −15V rail sources the
  negative swing.
  - Math: V_out = −(V_in − 2.5) → 2.5V→0, 0→+2.5, 5→−2.5.
  - Full range: −12.75V … +12.75V via DAC code (× the GainOffset M).

  No extra circuit needed — the bipolar op-amp + the DAC crossing mid-scale
  gives you negative offset.

● Good eye — you're right that CenterOffset (inverting) + GainOffset (inverting)
  = two inversions that cancel. But here's the key: that does NOT break your 
  bipolar offset, and you don't need another amp. Let me trace it.

  The inversions just flip the sign convention — bipolarity is preserved

  Trace the offset path:

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │    Stage     │    Operation    │       Result        │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ CenterOffset │ $-(V_o - 2.5)$  │ inverting           │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ GainOffset   │ $\times(-M)$    │ inverting → cancels │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ → after both │ $+M(V_o - 2.5)$ │ non-inverted        │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Summing amp  │ $\times(-1)$    │ inverting (3rd)     │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Final offset │ $-M(V_o - 2.5)$ │ net inverting       │
  └──────────────┴─────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  So the bipolar capability comes from (V_o − 2.5) — which is bipolar because
  the DAC crosses mid-scale (2.5V) — NOT from the inversions. The inversions
  only decide which DAC half gives + vs −:

  - $V_o < 2.5$ (code < 0x8000) → positive offset
  - $V_o > 2.5$ (code > 0x8000) → negative offset

  Offset is still fully bipolar. ✅

  So — no extra amp, no non-inverting stage needed

  - You do NOT need another inverting amplifier.
  - You do NOT need to make one non-inverting (which would mean a different
  topology / different sub-sheet — and you're right, that'd break the reuse).
  - The negative offset is produced by the op-amps' −15V rail swinging the
  output below ground — inherent, no added circuit.

  The only consequence: firmware sign mapping

  The inversion count just sets which code = which sign. Firmware handles it:
  target_offset → DAC_code:   code = 0x8000 − (target_offset / (M × step))
  i.e. for positive offset, code below 0x8000; for negative, code above. One
  line in firmware — zero hardware change.

  Signal path matches (so they add correctly)

  Signal: CenterSignal(inv) → Filter(non-inv) → Mux(non-inv) → GainSignal(inv) →
  Sum(inv) = 3 inversions, same as offset. Both net-inverting → they combine
  consistently at the summing amp, and the offset correctly shifts the signal.
  The final output is inverted vs the DAC — irrelevant for symmetric waves, and
  for sawtooth you just flip the wavetable in firmware.

  Bottom line

  - Cancellation is fine — bipolar offset still works (from V_o − 2.5, not the
  inversions)
  - No extra amp, no non-inverting stage — keep your reused sub-sheets as-is
  - Just map the sign in firmware (code <0x8000 = +, >0x8000 = −)

  Now let me add the centering math + negative-offset + this inversion analysis
  to the Quarto.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Yes to all three — and you've got the full picture now:

  1. Bipolar offset ✓

  The centering gives ±2.5V (both regions) before gain → after ×M, the offset
  spans the full −12.75V … +12.75V. Positive and negative, controlled by the DAC
  code crossing 2.5V. ✓

  2. Final output is 180° inverted ✓

  Each path has 3 inversions → net inverting:
  - Signal: CenterSignal(inv) → Filter(non-inv) → Mux(non-inv) → GainSignal(inv)
  → Summing(inv) = 3
  - Offset: CenterOffset(inv) → GainOffset(inv) → Summing(inv) = 3

  Both invert together (consistently), so the summing amp inverts the whole
  thing 180°. The waveform shape and the offset relationship are perfectly
  preserved — it's just globally flipped. ✓

  3. Yes — fix it in software, and that's the right call ✅

  Flip the wavetable in firmware — it's free:
  // store/compute the table pre-inverted (negate around the 0x8000 center)
  dac_sample = 0xFFFF - wavetable[i];   // or store table already inverted
  - For sine / square / triangle → inversion is invisible (an upside-down sine
  is still a sine), so you don't even have to do anything
  - For sawtooth → just flip the ramp direction in the table
  - Zero runtime cost if you bake the inversion into the stored table

  4. Software flip vs adding an inverting op-amp

  ┌────────┬─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │        │        Software flip        │      Hardware unity inverter      │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cost   │ free (you make the          │ +1 op-amp + 2 resistors + space + │
  │        │ wavetable anyway)           │  a ±15V load                      │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Result │ perfect                     │ perfect                           │
  └────────┴─────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  Recommendation: software flip — save the component. Since you generate the
  waveform digitally, inverting it is trivial; adding a hardware inverter just
  to undo a 180° flip is a wasted part. So yes — leave the inversion, handle it 
  in firmware, reduce component count and space.

  You can add a unity-gain inverter if you ever want the phase hardware-correct
  (it's a valid, clean option) — but for your goal of fewer parts, don't. It's
  redundant when the wavetable can be pre-inverted.

  Bottom line

  - ✓ Bipolar offset (DAC crossing 2.5V)
  - ✓ Output is 180° inverted (3 inversions/path), signal + offset flipped
  together
  - ✓ Correct it in software (flip/pre-invert the wavetable) → saves the 
  component & space
  - Adding an inverter op-amp is optional/unnecessary — your call, but software
  is the lean choice

  So your instinct is right: keep it lean, handle the phase in firmware.
  Symmetric waves won't even notice; sawtooth just needs a flipped table.

  ┌───────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐

  └───────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

❯ 2 chaneel dds and 1 osclil. where in 2 dds mode no offset                     

● Clever idea — and yes, it's feasible, because your offset path is already a
  near-complete second channel (16-bit DAC + centering + gain bank). But it
  needs a few honest additions. Let me lay it out, then I'll document
  everything.

  2-channel concept

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │     Mode     │ Signal DAC  │       Offset DAC        │      Output      │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ 1ch + offset │ waveform    │ DC offset               │ BNC1 = signal +  │
  │              │             │                         │ offset           │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ 2ch DDS      │ channel 1   │ channel 2 (waveform, no │ BNC1 = ch1, BNC2 │
  │              │             │  offset)                │  = ch2           │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Oscilloscope │ SAR ref /   │ —                       │ scope            │
  │              │ idle        │                         │                  │
  └──────────────┴─────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  What you must add

  1. A routing disconnect on the offset→summing path ⚠️ — in 2ch mode, channel 2
  must NOT be summed into channel 1, so you have to physically break that
  connection. This switch carries the ±12.75V GainOffset output, so it can't be 
  CD4066/CD4051 (±8V) — use a relay (MCU-driven) or a ±15V solid-state switch 
  (DG419).
    - Offset mode: closed → offset adds to ch1
    - 2ch mode: open → ch1 clean, GainOffset → BNC2
  2. Channel-2 output buffer + BNC2 (an inverting-unity stage like ch1), fed
  from GainOffset.
  3. (Optional) a filter for ch2 — the offset path has no Sallen-Key, so ch2 is
  the raw DAC staircase unless you add a second OutputFilter instance.

  Caveats (be aware)

  - ch2 is unfiltered unless you add a filter sub-sheet on the offset path
  - 2ch DDS doubles the ISR work (two phase accumulators + two SPI writes) →
  lower max frequency in 2ch mode (same tradeoff as your BOTH mode)
  - In 2ch mode both DACs are busy → the SAR scope can't run, so scope falls
  back to the internal 10-bit ADC
  - The relay/DG419 is MCU-commanded (digital), so it doesn't break your "no 
  manual switch" rule

  Firmware

  2ch mode = two independent phase accumulators (one per DAC), offset forced to
  0, routing switch opened. The signal DAC drives ch1, the offset DAC drives
  ch2.

  values, inverting-unity final stage, phase handling, and this 2-channel
  option) into the files.

❯ is 4.7k for summign amp okay than 10k? 

● Yes — 4.7k is perfectly fine for the summing amp. For a unity summer, only the
  ratio matters (Rf/Rin = 1), not the absolute value. 4.7k/4.7k/4.7k = unity,
  same as 10k would be.

  What the absolute value affects (all fine at 4.7k):

  ┌─────────────────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────┬────────────────┐
  │         Factor          │       4.7k        │   10k    │    Verdict     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Unity gain              │ Rf/Rin = 1 ✓      │ = 1 ✓    │ identical      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Loading on              │                   │          │ both trivial   │
  │ GainSignal/GainOffset   │ 4.7k load         │ 10k load │ for an op-amp  │
  │ outputs                 │                   │          │ (drives ≤2k    │
  │                         │                   │          │ easily)        │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │                         │                   │ slightly │ 4.7k           │
  │ Thermal noise           │ slightly lower    │  higher  │ marginally     │
  │                         │                   │          │ better         │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Bias-current offset     │ 0.14µV            │ 0.3µV    │ both           │
  │ (TL071 ~30pA)           │                   │          │ negligible     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Feedback-cap corner     │ 1/(2π·4.7k·47pF)  │ ≈ 340kHz │ both far above │
  │ (47pF)                  │ ≈ 720kHz          │          │  20kHz ✓       │
  └─────────────────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────┴────────────────┘

  So 4.7k is actually slightly better (lower noise) and sits comfortably in the
  1k–100k sweet spot. The only thing to keep equal is the three resistors 
  matching each other (R15=R17=R20) for true unity — which you have.

  Keep 4.7k. No need to change to 10k. (It's fine that your final inverting
  buffer uses 10k and the summing amp uses 4.7k — different stages don't need to
  match.)

  So: 4.7k summing amp ✓, unity confirmed, no change needed.

❯ why we use a NE5532? than a TL071?                                            

● Good question — it's about matching the op-amp to the job. NE5532 and TL071
  have opposite strengths:

  ┌───────────────────┬────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │       Spec        │  NE5532 (filter)   │  TL071 (buffer, centering,    │
  │                   │                    │             etc.)             │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Input type        │ bipolar            │ JFET                          │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Voltage noise     │ 5 nV/√Hz (quiet)   │ 18 nV/√Hz                     │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Input bias        │ ~200 nA            │ ~30 pA (tiny)                 │
  │ current           │                    │                               │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ GBW               │ 10 MHz             │ 3 MHz                         │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Slew rate         │ 9 V/µs             │ 13 V/µs                       │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Output drive      │ ±38 mA             │ ±20 mA                        │
  │                   │ (600Ω-rated)       │                               │
  ├───────────────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Channels          │ dual               │ single                        │
  └───────────────────┴────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  Why NE5532 in the Sallen-Key filter

  1. Low noise — the filter sits in the signal path, so its noise adds straight
  to the output. NE5532 is ~3.5× quieter than TL071. For a clean waveform, you
  want the quiet op-amp here.
  2. High GBW (10 MHz) — a multi-pole filter needs the op-amp's bandwidth well
  above the cutoff for an accurate response (less phase/gain error near fc). 10
  MHz gives big margin.
  3. It's a dual — your filter is 4-pole = 2 Sallen-Key stages = 2 op-amps, and
  one NE5532 package holds both. Tidy.
  4. Strong drive — easily drives the next stage (mux).

  Why TL071 (JFET) elsewhere — especially the buffer

  The DAC buffer sees the high-impedance R-2R ladder (10k). There, input bias 
  current is what matters:
  - TL071's 30 pA × 10k = 0.3 µV → nil
  - NE5532's 200 nA × 10k = 2 mV offset

  So at high-Z nodes (buffer), TL071's JFET input wins. NE5532's strength (low
  noise) is wasted there, and its weakness (bias current) bites.

  The principle (the real reason for the split)

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬───────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │              Node               │   Pick    │         Because          │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ High-impedance (DAC buffer)     │ TL071     │ near-zero bias current → │
  │                                 │ (JFET)    │  no offset               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Signal-path / filter (low-Z,    │ NE5532    │ low noise + high GBW +   │
  │ noise matters)                  │           │ dual                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴───────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  Honest note for your application

  For a function generator with a ±2.5V signal, the filter noise difference is 
  small (both give µV-level noise, negligible vs ±2.5V) — so TL071/TL072 in the
  filter would also work. NE5532 is just the better default for a filter (it's
  the classic low-noise/high-GBW audio-filter part, and it's dual). The split
  that really matters is the buffer — there TL071's low bias current is
  genuinely necessary.

  TL;DR

  - NE5532 in the filter → low noise, high GBW (filter accuracy), dual (4-pole
  in 1 chip)
  - TL071 in the buffer/centering → JFET low bias current for high-impedance
  nodes
  - Rule: JFET-input for high-Z, low-noise bipolar for the signal-path filter

  Both are ±15V, both SL-available — you're using each where it's strongest.

❯ what is th epurpose fo the 22pF in th eamp de amsp [Image #100]?              
  ⎿  [Image #100]

● C62 (22pF across the 51k feedback R110) is a feedback compensation cap — its
  job is stability, with HF noise roll-off as a bonus.

  Why it's needed (stability)

  The inverting amp's summing node has parasitic capacitance:
  - The CD4066 switches (~5–10pF each, ×8) on the gain bank
  - Stray wiring + the gain resistors

  That node capacitance, combined with the 51k feedback resistor, forms an extra
  pole that adds phase lag around the loop. Too much phase lag → the amp peaks,
  rings, or oscillates. C62 puts a small capacitor in the feedback that
  restores phase margin at high frequency → the stage stays stable. This is
  especially important in a gain bank because the CD4066 switches add real
  capacitance at the summing node.

  The bonus (HF roll-off)

  C62 also limits the stage's high-frequency gain:
  $$f_c = \frac{1}{2\pi R_{110} C_{62}} = \frac{1}{2\pi (51\text
  k)(22\text{pF})} \approx 142\ \text{kHz}$$

  So above ~142kHz the gain drops, which:
  - suppresses high-frequency noise
  - knocks down CD4066 switching glitches
  - is well above your 20kHz signal → no effect on the waveform

  Why 22pF (not 47pF like U7)?

  The value scales with the feedback resistor:
  - U7 (Rf = 10k) → 47pF → ~339kHz corner
  - This stage (R110 = 51k) → 22pF → ~142kHz corner

  Bigger Rf needs a smaller cap for a similar corner. Both are chosen to sit
  above the signal band but below the op-amp's GBW, giving stability without
  touching the signal.

  TL;DR

  - Primary: stability — cancels the pole made by the summing-node capacitance
  (CD4066 switches + stray) and the 51k feedback → no ringing/oscillation
  - Bonus: ~142kHz roll-off → less HF noise + switching glitches
  - Doesn't affect the signal (corner ≫ 20kHz)
  - 22pF (vs 47pF on U7) because the feedback R is larger here

  So it's the small "keep-it-stable" cap every inverting amp with a
  high-impedance/switched input wants — leave it in.

● Keep both — C48 and C52 are the same feedback compensation caps. Don't remove
  them.

  ┌───────────┬──────────────────────────────┬───────────┬─────────────────┐
  │    Cap    │            Stage             │ Feedback  │    Corner =     │
  │           │                              │     R     │    1/(2πRC)     │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ C48       │ U16 summing amp              │ R15 =     │ ~720 kHz        │
  │ (47pF)    │                              │ 4.7k      │                 │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ C52       │ U9 inverting (R26=R13=10k →  │ R13 = 10k │ ~339 kHz        │
  │ (47pF)    │ ×−1)                         │           │                 │
  └───────────┴──────────────────────────────┴───────────┴─────────────────┘

  Both corners are far above your 20kHz signal → zero effect on the waveform.
  Their job:

  - Stability — each inverting amp's summing node has stray/input capacitance;
  the feedback cap restores phase margin → no ringing or oscillation
  - Bonus — small HF roll-off → less noise/glitch

  Removing them gains nothing useful (your signal is nowhere near 339–720 kHz)
  and risks ringing. So keep them.

  General rule (so you don't have to ask each time)

  ▎ Every inverting op-amp with resistive feedback gets a small feedback cap 
  ▎ (≈10–50pF) for stability. Keep them all — they're cheap insurance, and their
  ▎ corner sits above your signal band by design.

  You've already sized them right (47pF on the 4.7k–10k feedbacks, 22pF on the
  51k feedback — bigger R → smaller cap, similar corner).

  Verdict: keep every one of these comp caps (47pF, 22pF). Don't remove. They're
  correct as-is.

● You're right — the dual-rail version is fiddly (the −rail NPN sensing into a
  +5V comparator needs a pull-up + clamp + a second threshold, which is
  confusing). Let me give you a clear, simpler protection that actually works,
  then note the full dual-rail if you really want it.

  Recommended (clear): +rail sense + internal limit + clamps

  The TL071 already current-limits internally (~25mA) on any short (both
  polarities) — so the op-amp survives a negative-output short on its own. You
  only need the +rail PNP sense for the smart-shutdown/reporting on the common
  case. This drops Q2/R28/R30 and the −rail mess.

  Explicit wiring (+15V sensor — Q1 BC557)

  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ VTH (≈0.5V)   │ U3A pin 3 (IN+)                                  │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ U3A out pin 1 │ → FAULT                                          │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ FAULT         │ 10k pull-up → +5V, and → PD3/INT1                │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ U3 power      │ pin 8 → +5V, pin 4 → GND                         │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Threshold divider: +5V → 100k → VTH → 11k → GND  (≈ 0.5V)

   +15V ──[R27 30Ω]──●──────────► U9 pin7 (V+)
          │          │ node P15
     Q1 e─┘     [Rb 1k]
    BC557      b──────┘
          c
          │
        node A ──────► U3A pin2 (IN−)
          │            U3A pin3 (IN+) ◄── VTH (0.5V)
        [R29 10k]      U3A pin1 (out) ──► FAULT
          │                                 │
         GND                          [10k → +5V]
                                            │
                                          PD3/INT1

  How it trips: positive-output short → op-amp's +15V current spikes → R27 drop
  > 0.6V → Q1 conducts → node A rises past 0.5V → U3A output LOW → PD3 fault.
  Normal: node A = 0V, output high.

  Negative-output faults

  Handled by the TL071's internal ~25mA limit + R_out + clamps — the op-amp
  survives, just no smart-report on that polarity. Adequate for a bench
  instrument.

  If you insist on full dual-rail (the −rail part)

  The −rail NPN (Q2) can't make a 0-to-positive signal — it pulls down. To use
  it with a +5V LM393 you must:
  - R30 → +5V (not GND) so node B sits at +5V and drops on fault
  - add a clamp diode (anode→GND, cathode→node B) so it can't go below −0.7V
  - give U3B a second threshold ≈4.5V (node B → pin5 IN+, 4.5V → pin6 IN−), out
  LOW when node B < 4.5V
  - both outputs (pin1, pin7) wire-OR → FAULT

  That's the complexity that made it unclear. For clarity I'd do the +rail-only 
  version above — it's clean, the op-amp self-protects on the other side, and
  you keep the fault-reporting feature.

  Which do you want — the clean +rail-only (recommended), or shall I draw the 
  full dual-rail explicitly?

  ┌────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────┐
  │    Rail    │     On fault      │      Comparator      │     Output     │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┼────────────────┤

  └────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────────┴────────────────┘

  Both outputs are open-collector, wired together → either one pulls FAULT (PD3)
  low.

  Key changes vs your current placement

  1. R30 → +5V (you had it to GND) — so node B sits at +5V and drops on fault
  2. Add D_clamp (cathode→node B, anode→GND) — keeps node B ≥ −0.7V for the

  → firmware mutes/reports.

❯ [Image #103] [Image #104] is this correct?i have no idea abotu how the

● Let me first explain how the transistor helps (your main question), then check
  the wiring — because there are a couple of things to verify.

  What the transistor does (its 2 jobs)

  The comparator (LM393) runs on +5V/GND, so its inputs can only see 0–3.5V. But
  your overcurrent signal sits up at +15V (or down at −15V) — the comparator
  can't look there directly. The transistor fixes that:

  1. Threshold detector: it turns ON when the voltage across the sense resistor
  exceeds ~0.6V (its base-emitter turn-on). 0.6V ÷ 30Ω = 20mA → that's your trip
  point.
  2. Level-shifter: once on, it pushes a collector current through a resistor to
  ground, creating a ground-referenced voltage the +5V comparator can read.

  op-amp current → [Rs 30Ω at +15V] → tiny 0.6V drop (stuck up at +15V)
                            │
                      TRANSISTOR  ── turns on at 0.6V, copies the event down to
  ground
                            │
                      collector → [Rc] → GND  → ground-referenced voltage (0–few
  V)
                            │
                       COMPARATOR (reads this, +5V world) → clean HIGH/LOW → PD3

  So the transistor translates a +15V-referenced overcurrent into a 
  ground-referenced voltage the comparator can compare. Without it, the LM393
  couldn't see the high-side current.

  ⚠️ The signal flow that must be right: COLLECTOR → comparator

  The comparator must read the collector (the node going through R29 to GND) —
  NOT the base. The base is the sense connection; the collector is the output.

  - Base → senses the rail (the Rs node / op-amp supply side)
  - Collector → R29 → GND → this node goes to the comparator

  In your +15V schematic, double-check that U3A pin 2 connects to Q1's collector
  (top of R29), not to Q1's base. If it's on the base, move it to the
  collector.

  ⚠️ Things to fix / verify

  1. −rail pull-up (R28 = 30Ω) → should be 10k. The resistor from +5V to the NPN
  collector is the pull-up; at 30Ω it would dump 5V/30Ω = 167mA when Q2
  conducts. Make it 10k.
  2. Op-amp supply through the sense resistors: U9 pin 7 (V+) must connect to
  the bottom of R27 (so the op-amp's +15V current flows through R27 = what
  you're sensing). Same on the −rail: U9 pin 4 (V−) → top of R30 (the 30Ω).
  3. Collector → comparator (both rails), base → sense node, emitter → the rail.

  Correct roles (verify against your symbols)

  Q1 BC557 (+rail): emitter → +15V · base → R27-bottom (op-amp V+ side) via
  R111(1k) · collector → R29 → GND → U3A IN− · VTH_HI(0.5V) → U3A IN+

  Q2 BC547 (−rail): emitter → −15V · base → R30-top (op-amp V− side) via
  R116(1k) · collector → R28(make 10k) → +5V, + D1 clamp → U3B IN+ ·
  VTH_LO(4.5V) → U3B IN−

  Summary

  - Transistor = threshold detector (0.6V≈20mA) + level-shifter so the +5V
  comparator can see the rail current
  - Comparator reads the COLLECTOR, not the base — verify this
  - −rail +5V pull-up = 10k, not 30Ω
  - Op-amp V+/V− must tap through R27/R30 so their current is what's sensed

  Fix those and it's correct. The thresholds (0.5V, 4.5V) and clamp look right.

● I see the confusion — let me clear up the one core idea, because the
  transistor wiring is backwards right now.

  The key idea: the OP-AMP's current flows through the 30Ω. The transistor just 
  watches the voltage across it.

  The 30Ω (R27) is not in series with the transistor's emitter — it's in series
  with the op-amp's power pin. The op-amp's supply current flows through R27.
  The transistor sits across R27 (emitter on one end, base on the other) and
  "watches" the voltage. When that voltage hits 0.6V (= 20mA), it turns on.

  So:
  - Emitter → directly to +15V (the TOP of R27)
  - R27 (30Ω) → from +15V down to the op-amp's V+ pin
  - Base → the op-amp-V+ side (BOTTOM of R27), via R111
  - Op-amp current flows through R27 → that's what's sensed

  Right now you have the emitter going through R27 (emitter at the bottom) —
  that's backwards, and the PNP can never turn on that way.

  Correct +15V sensor (fix the emitter)

          +15V ──┬───────────────────────►  Q1 EMITTER   (emitter DIRECTLY on
  +15V)
                 │
              [R27 30Ω]      ← op-amp current flows through this
                 │
              node P15 ──────────────────►  U9 pin 7 (V+)   ← op-amp supply taps
  HERE
                 │
              [R111 1k]
                 │
            Q1 BASE
                 │
            Q1 COLLECTOR ── node A ──[R29 10k]── GND
                                    └─────────►  U3A pin 2 (IN−)
     VTH_HI 0.5V ─────────────────────────────►  U3A pin 3 (IN+)

  What happens: op-amp draws current → flows +15V→R27→P15→op-amp → P15 drops
  below +15V by I×30Ω. Emitter (+15V) vs base (P15): when the gap hits 0.6V
  (20mA), Q1 turns on → collector pushes current through R29 → node A rises →
  comparator trips. ✓

  Correct −15V sensor (same fix, mirrored)

            Q2 COLLECTOR ── node B ──[R28 10k]── +5V
                                    ├──[D1]── GND   (cathode→node B, anode→GND)
                                    └─────────►  U3B pin 5 (IN+)
            Q2 BASE ──[R116 1k]── node M15
            Q2 EMITTER ──────────► −15V          (emitter DIRECTLY on −15V)

            −15V ──[R30 30Ω]── node M15 ──────►  U9 pin 4 (V−)   ← op-amp supply
  taps HERE
     VTH_LO 4.5V ──────────────────────────────►  U3B pin 6 (IN−)

  The two fixes you need

  1. Emitter → directly to the rail (+15V for Q1, −15V for Q2) — NOT through the
  30Ω.
  2. The 30Ω goes between the rail and the op-amp's supply pin, and the base 
  taps the op-amp-supply side of the 30Ω (via the 1k).
  3. Crucial: the op-amp's V+ (pin7) and V− (pin4) must connect to node P15 / 
  node M15 (the bottom/top of the sense resistors) — so the op-amp's current is
  what flows through them. If U9's supplies go straight to ±15V, the 30Ω senses
  nothing.

  What you already got right ✓

  - Collector → comparator ✓
  - R28 = 10k pull-up ✓
  - Thresholds (0.5V, 4.5V) ✓
  - D1 clamp present ✓

  So the only change: emitter directly to the rail, 30Ω between the rail and the
  op-amp's power pin, base on the op-amp-power side. Then the transistor
  correctly "watches" the op-amp's supply current. Want me to redraw it with
  your exact R-numbers placed?

❯ but my power suppyl is [Image #107]                                           
  ⎿  [Image #107]

● Important — you're right to flag this. The 30Ω must go in the OUTPUT op-amp's 
  individual supply pin, NOT in the global ±15V rail. Otherwise it would (a)
  drop voltage for every op-amp and (b) sense total current, not the output's.

  How to tap it correctly

  Your +15V is a global rail feeding everything. Leave that alone. For the
  output buffer only, branch off the rail through R27 to just that op-amp's V+ 
  pin:

   GLOBAL +15V ──┬─────────────────────────► all OTHER op-amps (filter, gain,
  centering)  [direct]
                 │
                 ├─────────────────────────► Q1 EMITTER
                 │
              [R27 30Ω]      ← only the OUTPUT buffer's current flows here
                 │
          node OUT_VP ───────────────────────► OUTPUT BUFFER pin 7 (V+)   ← ONLY
  this op-amp
                 │
              [R111 1k] ──► Q1 BASE
                 │
            Q1 COLLECTOR ──► R29 → GND → comparator

  Same on the negative side:
   OUTPUT BUFFER pin 4 (V−) ──► node OUT_VN ──[R30 30Ω]──► GLOBAL −15V
   (Q2 emitter → −15V, base → OUT_VN via R116)
   all other op-amps → −15V direct

  Why this works

  - Only the output stage's supply current flows through R27/R30 → and that
  current reflects the BNC load (a short makes the output op-amp draw/sink lots
  of current → R27/R30 drop spikes → trip).
  - Every other op-amp connects to the global ±15V directly → no voltage drop,
  no false trips.

  In KiCad

  - Don't connect the output buffer's V+ pin to the +15V global net.
  - Instead: +15V (global) → R27 → a local label (e.g. OUT_VP) → output buffer
  pin 7.
  - Q1 emitter → +15V (global), Q1 base → OUT_VP (via R111).
  - Same pattern: output buffer pin 4 → OUT_VN → R30 → −15V (global); Q2 emitter
  → −15V.

  So the sense resistors sit on a short local branch to the one op-amp you're
  protecting (the output driver), tapped off the global rail. The global rail
  stays clean for all the other stages.

  Which op-amp gets the sense?

  The final output buffer — the inverting-unity stage that drives the BNC. Its
  supply current is the one that reflects the output load, so that's the only
  op-amp whose V+/V− you route through R27/R30.

  So: global rail untouched; R27/R30 only in the output buffer's own V+/V− 
  branch. That resolves your concern — you're not putting 30Ω in series with the
  whole supply, just the one stage you're protecting.

※ recap: You're designing the ATmega32A function generator's analog output stage in KiCad. Right now you're wiring the dual-rail overcurrent protection. Next: put the 30Ω sense resistors in the output buffer's own ±15V branch (tapped off the global rail), not in the shared rail.

● Big progress — the IUG_P_V_IN / IUG_N_V_IN labels correctly route the op-amp's
  supply through R27/R30, emitter→rail and collector→comparator are right. But 
  one connection is still wrong on both rails: the base resistor is tied to the 
  wrong end.

  The rule: base & emitter must STRADDLE the sense resistor

  The transistor measures the voltage across R27 (or R30). So:
  - Emitter → one end (the rail, +15V)
  - Base → the other end (the op-amp side = IUG_P_V_IN)

  Right now your base (via R111) is on the +15V side — the SAME end as the 
  emitter. Both ends at +15V → it measures nothing → Q1 never turns on.

     WRONG (what you have):              RIGHT (the fix):

     +15V ──┬── emitter                  +15V ──┬── emitter
            │                                   │
          [R27]                              [R27]
            │                                   │
       IUG_P_V_IN                          IUG_P_V_IN ──┬── base (via R111)
            │                                   │       └──[R111 1k]──┘
     +15V ──[R111]── base  ✗              (base straddles R27 with emitter) ✓

  The two fixes

  +15V rail (image 109): move R111's top from +15V → IUG_P_V_IN (the bottom of
  R27). So base senses the op-amp side; emitter stays on +15V. Now they straddle
  R27.

  −15V rail (image 110): move R116's end from −15V → IUG_N_V_IN (the top of
  R30). So base senses the op-amp side; emitter stays on −15V. They straddle
  R30.

  Why this makes it work

  - +rail: emitter = +15V, base = IUG_P_V_IN = (+15V − I·R27). When op-amp
  current makes that gap = 0.6V (20mA) → Q1 turns on → collector → R29 →
  comparator. ✓
  - −rail: emitter = −15V, base = IUG_N_V_IN = (−15V + I·R30). When gap = 0.6V →
  Q2 turns on. ✓

  What's already correct ✓

  - R27/R30 in the op-amp's own supply branch (via IUG labels) ✓
  - Emitter → rail ✓
  - Collector → comparator ✓
  - R28 = 10k pull-up, D1 clamp, thresholds, +5V comparator power ✓

  So the only change

  - R111 → IUG_P_V_IN (not +15V)
  - R116 → IUG_N_V_IN (not −15V)

  That puts the base on the opposite side of the sense resistor from the
  emitter, so the transistor finally "sees" the op-amp's supply current.
  Everything else is right.

● This part is correct ✓ — the wire-OR fault output is wired right:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │                 Element                  │            Status            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ U3A out (pin 1) + U3B out (pin 7) tied   │ ✓ wire-OR (both              │
  │ together                                 │ open-collector)              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ R117 (10k) pull-up to +5V                │ ✓                            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ node → PD3                               │ ✓                            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ U3C: pin 8 → +5V, pin 4 → GND (LM393     │ ✓                            │
  │ power)                                   │                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  Logic check:
  - Normal: both comparator outputs high-Z → R117 pulls PD3 to +5V (HIGH)
  - Fault (either rail): that comparator pulls the line LOW → PD3 goes LOW
  - → active-LOW fault, matches your INT1 falling-edge config ✓

  PD3 swings a clean 0–5V (open-collector pulls to ~0V, R117 pulls to +5V) —
  safe for the ATmega input.

  This completes the protection output — just confirm the earlier two fixes are 
  done:

  1. R111 → IUG_P_V_IN (not +15V) — base straddles R27
  2. R116 → IUG_N_V_IN (not −15V) — base straddles R30

  If those base-resistor moves are done, the whole dual-rail protection is 
  complete and correct:
  - ✅ Sense resistors in the output op-amp's own supply branch (IUG labels)
  - ✅ Transistors straddle the sense resistors (emitter→rail, base→op-amp side)
  - ✅ Collector → comparator
  - ✅ Thresholds (0.5V / 4.5V), D1 clamp, +5V pull-up
  - ✅ Wire-OR → PD3 (active-LOW)

  Nice work — the protection block is essentially finished. Want to move on to
  documenting the final protection wiring in the files, or to the firmware
  (fault ISR + the PROTECT/WARN/OFF modes)?

  - U3C: pin 8 → +5V, pin 4 → GND ✓

  The one principle that makes it work

  Emitter and base sit on OPPOSITE ends of the sense resistor (emitter→rail,
  base→op-amp side via the 1k). That's what lets the transistor "watch" the
  op-amp's supply current. If that's true on both rails, the resistor network + 
  transistor placement is correct.

  Quick sanity values

  - Trip ≈ 0.6V / 30Ω = 20mA per rail
  - Transistors dissipate <10mW → cold
  - Sense is on the output buffer's own supply branch (IUG labels) → doesn't
  load the global rail or other op-amps

❯ give a proper port name for this fault → PD3 / INT1 from our design. also i   
  hope this wont fry the chip later on feeding                                  

❯ shoil i place 6n137 just for saftyl?                                          

● Net name

  Following your convention PD<pin>_<BLOCK>_<SIGNAL>:

  PD3_PROT_FAULT

  (PD3, the PROTection block, the FAULT signal). Clear and PCB-traceable, like
  your other nets.

  Will it fry the chip? — No ✓

  The fault line is already a clean 0–5V digital signal, bounded by design:
  - The LM393 output is open-collector → it can only pull to GND (0V) or float
  - R117 (10k) pulls it to +5V when not pulling low
  - → PD3 only ever sees 0V or +5V

  That's exactly what a 5V ATmega pin expects — no over-voltage, no negative 
  voltage, no risk. The dangerous ±15V is all on the input side of the LM393
  (and the transistors); the MCU only ever touches the LM393's safe 0–5V output.

  Do you need a 6N137 here? — No

  The 6N137 is for the oscilloscope comparator (PD2), where the NE5532 swings
  ±13V — there you need the opto to keep ±13V off the MCU pin.

  But the protection fault line is different: the LM393 already does the 
  level-shift — it converts the ±15V rail-current sense into a tidy 0–5V
  open-collector output. So PD3 never sees anything but 0–5V. An opto would be 
  redundant — the LM393 + 5V supply is the protection.

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────┐
  │   MCU pin   │           Source           │ Voltage seen │ Needs opto? │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ PD2 (scope) │ NE5532 ±13V                │ ±13V raw     │ Yes → 6N137 │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ PD3 (fault) │ LM393 (+5V) open-collector │ 0–5V only    │ No          │
  └─────────────┴────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────┘

  Optional cheap insurance (not required)

  If you want belt-and-suspenders, drop a 1kΩ series resistor between the fault
  node and PD3. It does nothing in normal operation but limits current into the
  pin's internal clamp diodes if there's ever a transient or you accidentally
  drive PD3 as an output. ~₹1, harmless. But it's truly optional — the line is
  already safe.

  Summary

  - Net name: PD3_PROT_FAULT
  - Won't fry the chip — LM393 open-collector + 5V pull-up = 0–5V only, exactly
  MCU-safe
  - No 6N137 needed — the LM393 already isolates/level-shifts; the opto is only
  for the ±13V scope comparator (PD2)
  - Optional 1k series R to PD3 as cheap transient insurance

  So you're good — feed PD3 directly from the LM393 wire-OR node (the
  PD3_PROT_FAULT net), no opto required.

  And yes — make it a sub-sheet (it's now repeated)

  Since the protection is identical for ch1 and ch2, make a reused Protection 
  sub-sheet — same pattern as your DAC / Centering / gain_stage. Instantiate it
  twice.

  ┌────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │ Sheet name │         File         │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ ProtectCh1 │ protection.kicad_sch │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ ProtectCh2 │ protection.kicad_sch │
  └────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  Sub-sheet interface

  ┌─────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Hier.   │         Type          │        Connects (per instance)        │
  │   pin   │                       │                                       │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ IUG_P   │ Passive               │ buffer V+ node (IUG_P_V_IN for ch1,   │
  │         │                       │ _2 for ch2)                           │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ IUG_N   │ Passive               │ buffer V− node (IUG_N_V_IN / _2)      │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ FAULT   │ Output                │ → wire-OR on parent → PD3             │
  │         │ (open-collector)      │                                       │
  └─────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  Power (+15V, −15V, +5V, GND) via global symbols inside — no pins. Contains:
  R27/R30 (30Ω sense), Q1/Q2, R111/R116 (1k), R29/R28 (10k), D1 clamp,
  thresholds, LM393 (one dual chip per instance → 2 total).

  Combining the two fault lines

  Both instances' FAULT outputs are open-collector → tie them together on the
  parent → PD3:

  ProtectCh1 FAULT ──┐
                     ●──[R117 10k → +5V]──► PD3_PROT_FAULT
  ProtectCh2 FAULT ──┘

  → Put R117 on the parent sheet (one shared pull-up for the wire-OR), not
  inside the sub-sheet. Either channel's fault pulls PD3 low. (If you left a
  pull-up inside each, you'd get two in parallel = 5k — still works, just
  cleaner to share one.)

  One-line vs per-channel fault

  - Shared PD3 (recommended, simple): "any channel overcurrent" → one interrupt,
  firmware mutes/reports.
  - Separate pins (if you want to know which channel faulted): give each
  instance its own FAULT pin → PD3 (ch1) and another spare pin (ch2). Costs one
  more pin.

  For a campus build, shared PD3 is fine.

  Summary

  - ✅ Replicate protection for ch2 (its BNC can be shorted)
  - ✅ Make protection.kicad_sch a sub-sheet, instantiate ×2 (ProtectCh1,
  ProtectCh2)
  - Interface: IUG_P, IUG_N, FAULT (+ globals for power)
  - Wire-OR both FAULT pins + one R117 pull-up on the parent → PD3

  So: yes to both — replicate it, and yes, sub-sheet it (it's repeated now). The
  IUG_P/IUG_N taps you added make each instance drop cleanly onto each
  channel's buffer.

❯ [Image #116] which ports shoil i use                                          
  ⎿  [Image #116]
  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ both PD3 or seperate ones to identification easier                            

❯ how abtu PD2 and PD3                                                          

❯ also for 2nd chanel voltag efollwo seperation forem here okay? [Image #117]   
  ⎿  [Image #117]

● Three things — let me take them in order.

  1 & 2. Fault pins — NOT PD2 (it's taken)

  PD2 is the oscilloscope SAR comparator (6N137 → INT0) — don't use it for a
  fault. The ATmega32A only has 3 external-interrupt pins: INT0=PD2 (scope), 
  INT1=PD3, INT2=PB2 (your MUX_RCLK). So PD3 (INT1) is the only fault-interrupt 
  pin available.

  Recommended:
  - Combined fault → PD3 (INT1) — wire-OR both channels' FAULT → fast interrupt
  → mute
  - For per-channel ID: route each channel's FAULT also to a spare polled pin —
  PC0 (ch1), PC1 (ch2). The PD3 ISR reads PC0/PC1 to see which channel tripped.

  ProtectCh1 FAULT ──┬──────────────► PC0  (poll: ch1 faulted?)
                     ●──┐
  ProtectCh2 FAULT ──┬──┤
                     │  ●──[R117 10k→+5V]──► PD3/INT1  (combined trip)
                     └──────────────► PC1  (poll: ch2 faulted?)

  So: PD3 = the interrupt (combined), PC0/PC1 = which-channel ID (polled). Don't
  touch PD2. If you don't care which channel, skip PC0/PC1 and just mute both
  on PD3.

  3. Channel-2 tap from GainOffset OUT — point is fine, but you MUST add a 
  disconnect

  Tapping ch2 from GainOffset OUT (image 117) is the right point ✓. But there's
  a problem: GainOffset OUT also goes through R20 to the summing amp. In 2ch 
  mode you said "no offset" — so ch2 must NOT be summed into ch1.

  Right now R20 always connects GainOffset → summing amp, so in 2ch mode ch1 
  would be signal + ch2 (mixed). You need to break that path in 2ch mode:

  GainOffset OUT ──●──────────────────────► ch2 buffer (U30) → BNC2   [always]
                   │
              [SW / relay]   ◄── open in 2ch mode, closed in offset mode
                   │
                [R20 4.7k] ──► summing amp (offset into ch1)

  - Offset mode: SW closed → GainOffset adds DC offset to ch1
  - 2ch mode: SW open → ch1 = signal only, GainOffset = ch2 only

  The signal there is ±13V, so the switch must be a relay or a ±15V analog 
  switch (DG419) — NOT CD4066/CD4051 (±8V). MCU-controlled (digital), so it
  doesn't break your no-manual-switch rule.

  Summary

  - Fault interrupt → PD3 (INT1) (combined). PD2 is the scope — don't use it.
  Per-channel ID → poll PC0 / PC1.
  - ch2 tap from GainOffset OUT = correct, but add a relay/DG419 in the R20 
  (offset→summing) path so 2ch mode keeps ch1 clean.

  So your tap point is right; just insert the mode-disconnect before R20, and
  use PD3 + (optional) PC0/PC1 for faults.

✻ Churned for 4m 33s

❯ now we need to make this path right? [Image #118].                            
  ⎿  [Image #118]

● Yes — this is the oscilloscope input path. Here's the full component-level
  circuit, per our convention.

  Full chain (4 stages)

  ══ STAGE 1: Probe protection + 2.5V bias
  ══════════════════════════════════════

   PROBE ─[F1 100mA]─┬─[R_in 10k 1W]─┬──[R_sig 10k]──●  BIAS NODE
                     │               │               │
                  [MOV S14K275]   [D1]▲ →+5V          │   2.5V bias:
                     │            [D2]▼ →GND      +5V─[10k]─●─[10k]─GND
                    GND            (clamps)                 │ +[100nF]
                                                         BIAS NODE
     BIAS NODE ──►  NE5532-B pin 5 (+)    AND    ──►  PA0  (internal 10-bit ADC)

  ══ STAGE 2: NE5532-B comparator (open-loop)
  ═══════════════════════════════════

     BIAS NODE ───► pin 5 (+IN)
     DACV ────────► pin 6 (−IN)      ◄── SAR reference (buffered ladder output)
     pin 7 (OUT) ──► CMP_OUT  (±13V swing, open-loop)
     pin 8 → +15V,  pin 4 → −15V,  0.1µF decoupling each

  ══ STAGE 3: 6N137 isolation
  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════

     CMP_OUT ─[820Ω]─[1N4148]─►  6N137 pin 2 (anode)
                                6N137 pin 3 (cathode) → GND
     6N137:  pin 8 (VCC)→+5V · pin 7 (VE)→+5V · pin 5 (GND)→GND
             pin 6 (VO, open-coll) ─[4.7k → +5V]─► PD2

  ══ STAGE 4: ATmega
  ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

     PD2 / INT0  ◄── 6N137 output (SAR comparator result)
     PA0 (ADC0)  ◄── BIAS NODE (fast 10-bit path)

  IC specs (per convention)

  NE5532-B — comparator (open-loop), supply ±15V

  ┌───────┬─────────┬────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │  Pin  │  Name   │  Type  │         Connects         │
  ├───────┼─────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 5     │ +IN-B   │ Input  │ BIAS NODE (biased probe) │
  ├───────┼─────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 6     │ −IN-B   │ Input  │ DACV (SAR reference)     │
  ├───────┼─────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 7     │ OUT-B   │ Output │ → 820Ω → 1N4148 → 6N137  │
  ├───────┼─────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ 8 / 4 │ V+ / V− │ Power  │ +15V / −15V              │
  └───────┴─────────┴────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  (NE5532-A — the other half — is free; reserve for future.)

  6N137 — optocoupler, output side +5V

  ┌─────┬─────────────┬────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │ Pin │    Name     │        Type        │        Connects         │
  ├─────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 2   │ Anode       │ Input              │ 820Ω + 1N4148 ← CMP_OUT │
  ├─────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 3   │ Cathode     │ Input              │ GND                     │
  ├─────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 5   │ GND         │ Power              │ GND                     │
  ├─────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 6   │ VO          │ Output (open-coll) │ 4.7k → +5V, → PD2       │
  ├─────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 7   │ VE (enable) │ Input              │ +5V (enable — tie HIGH) │
  ├─────┼─────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ 8   │ VCC         │ Power              │ +5V                     │
  └─────┴─────────────┴────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ VE (pin 7) → +5V to enable the output (if it's LOW, the 6N137 output is
  disabled/high-Z). 0.1µF from pin 8 to pin 5.

  How the parts do their job

  - 1N4148 in series with the LED blocks the comparator's −13V swing → LED only
  lights on the +swing → protects the LED.
  - 820Ω sets ~14mA LED current at +13V.
  - 6N137 turns the ±13V comparator output into a clean 0–5V open-collector
  signal for PD2 — so the MCU never sees ±13V (this is why it's here, unlike the
  protection-fault line which is already safe).
  - DACV = the buffered signal-ladder output. In OSC mode the firmware drives
  the DAC with binary-search values; the comparator tells the MCU (via PD2)
  "probe > DACV?" → SAR converges in 16 steps.

  Net names (convention)

  - PD2_SCOPE_CMP (6N137 → PD2/INT0)
  - PA0_SCOPE_ADC (bias node → PA0)
  - DACV (shared SAR reference from the ladder buffer)

  Hierarchical?

  Single-use, but it's a coherent block — you can sub-sheet it as ScopeFrontEnd
  (scope_frontend.kicad_sch) for organization. Interface: PROBE (in), DACV (in,
  ref), PD2_SCOPE_CMP (out), PA0_SCOPE_ADC (out); power via globals. Optional —
  only one instance, so it's for tidiness.

  Safety note

  This front-end is bench-only, ≤30V, no mains — there's no galvanic isolation
  from your supplies (the 6N137 protects the MCU pin, not the user). Keep the F1
  fuse + MOV + clamps as the input guard.

  So yes — build this path: probe → protect/bias → NE5532-B (vs DACV) → 6N137 → 
  PD2, plus bias node → PA0. Want the SAR firmware (the 16-step binary search
  driving the DAC and reading PD2)?

✻ Churned for 3m 19s

❯ for sar why spedcidifcaly we need PD2. sicne offset path is purly digital we  
  can seprate is usign a simpel relay right? becuseother parts are nto easy to  
  ombyby what do you sujjest from sri lankan shpps specialy nilabra or tronic   
  or pitakotuwa                                                                 

● Two good questions.

  1. SAR doesn't actually NEED PD2

  The SAR algorithm polls the comparator — it sets a DAC value, waits, then
  reads the comparator pin, decides the bit, repeats. It never uses an interrupt
  for the conversion:

  for (bit = 15; bit >= 0; bit--) {
      set_dac_bit(bit);
      delay();
      if (read_comparator_pin()) ...   // just reads the GPIO
  }

  So the comparator can be on any free digital input pin — PD2 isn't required.

  Why PD2 was chosen: PD2 = INT0, which is handy if you later want a hardware 
  oscilloscope trigger (detect a signal edge → interrupt → start capture).
  That's the only reason to prefer PD2. For plain SAR sampling, any GPIO works —
  so you're free to move it (e.g. to a PC pin) if you need PD2 elsewhere.

  → Bottom line: keep it on PD2 if you want the trigger-interrupt option;
  otherwise put it on any free pin. Not a hard requirement.

  2. Relay for the 2ch disconnect — yes, and here's the SL parts

  Right — the mode disconnect (break GainOffset → summing amp in 2ch mode) is
  MCU-controlled, and a relay is the easy SL-available choice (handles ±13V on
  its contacts, unlike CD4066/CD4051; DG419 is hard to find here).

  Relay (tronic.lk / nilabra / pitakotuwa):
  - Songle SRD-05VDC-SL-C — the common blue-cube 5V SPDT relay. Everywhere in
  SL. Contacts rated way above your ±13V/few-mA signal. ~5ms switching — fine
  since mode changes are rare.

  Coil driver (don't drive a relay coil straight from a GPIO):
  GPIO ─[1k]─ base ┌ BC337 (or 2N2222)
                   │ emitter → GND
                   │ collector → relay coil → +5V
     flyback: 1N4148 across coil (cathode→+5V, anode→collector)
  - BC337 or 2N2222 (NOT BC547 — the Songle coil draws ~72mA, near BC547's 100mA
  limit; BC337/2N2222 handle it comfortably). All SL-common.
  - 1N4148 (or 1N4007) flyback diode across the coil — essential (kills the
  coil's switch-off spike).
  - 1k base resistor.

  Wiring the contact: put the relay's NO or NC contact in series with the
  GainOffset → R20 (summing) path:
  - Offset mode: contact closed → offset adds to ch1
  - 2ch mode: contact open → ch1 clean, GainOffset → ch2 only

  Parts list (all SL-available)

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │        Part        │          Use           │           Shop            │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ Songle             │ mode disconnect relay  │ tronic/nilabra/pitakotuwa │
  │ SRD-05VDC-SL-C     │ (5V SPDT)              │                           │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ BC337 or 2N2222    │ coil driver            │ common                    │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 1N4148 / 1N4007    │ flyback diode          │ common                    │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 1kΩ                │ base resistor          │ common                    │
  └────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  Summary

  - SAR doesn't need PD2 — it polls; any GPIO works. PD2/INT0 only earns its
  keep if you want a hardware trigger interrupt.
  - Relay for the 2ch disconnect: Songle SRD-05VDC-SL-C, driven by BC337/2N2222 
  + 1N4148 flyback + 1k, all from your usual SL shops. Contact in series with
  GainOffset→summing, open in 2ch mode.

✻ Sautéed for 4m 12s

● Yes — confirmed: the SAR uses an EXTERNAL comparator, the NE5532-B (one half
  of the NE5532 dual op-amp), run open-loop as a comparator. Not the ATmega's
  internal comparator.

  Why external (not the MCU's built-in comparator)

  The ATmega32A does have an internal analog comparator — but we don't use it
  because:
  1. Its pins are AIN0 = PB2, AIN1 = PB3 — both already used (PB2 = MUX_RCLK,
  PB3 = signal DAC latch).
  2. The external NE5532-B + 6N137 gives a clean, protected, fast comparison and
  isolates the ±13V swing from the MCU (the internal comparator would expose
  the pin directly).

  So the SAR comparison is:
  biased probe ─► NE5532-B (+)
  DACV (ref)  ─► NE5532-B (−)     ← external op-amp, open-loop = comparator
  NE5532-B out ─[820Ω+1N4148]─► 6N137 ─► PD2
  The MCU just reads PD2 (poll) during the 16-step binary search. External 
  comparator, confirmed.

  The relay you found — yes, that's the right one ✓

  Songle 5VDC Miniature Relay (SRD-05VDC-SL-C, 10A 250VAC/30VDC, Rs.100 at
  tronic.lk) — that's exactly the part for the 2ch disconnect. The 10A/30VDC
  contacts handle your ±13V signal trivially.

  Drive it with (all SL-common):
  GPIO ─[1k]─ base BC337 (or 2N2222)
              emitter → GND
              collector → relay coil → +5V
     flyback 1N4148 across the coil (cathode→+5V)
  Use one contact (NO or NC) in series with the GainOffset → summing-amp (R20) 
  path — open in 2ch mode, closed in offset mode.

  So, to confirm both:

  1. External comparator = NE5532-B (open-loop), result via 6N137 → PD2. ✓ Not
  the internal comparator (its pins are taken, and external + opto is safer).
  2. Relay = Songle 5VDC SRD-05VDC-SL-C (Rs.100) ✓ — driven by BC337/2N2222 +
  1N4148 flyback + 1k, contact in series with the offset→summing path.

  Both confirmed — you've got the right parts. Want me to draw the relay +
  driver wiring into the 2ch-disconnect spot, or add it to the docs?

✻ Churned for 4m 9s

❯ nice reason Why PD2 was chosen: PD2 = INT0, which is handy if you later want  
  a hardware oscilloscope trigger (detect a signal edge →                       
    interrupt → start capture). That's the only reason to prefer PD2.           

● Glad it landed. And the nice part: that trigger feature is basically free with
  what you've already built — no extra hardware.

  How the trigger would work (when you want it)

  The comparator + INT0 are already wired, so:

  1. Set the DAC (DACV) to your desired trigger level (e.g. 0V crossing, or any
  voltage)
  2. The NE5532-B comparator now outputs an edge the moment the probe crosses
  that level
  3. That edge hits PD2/INT0 → interrupt fires → start the capture at exactly
  that point

  // trigger setup (OSC mode)
  dac_write(trigger_level_code);     // DACV = trigger threshold
  MCUCR |= (1<<ISC01)|(1<<ISC00);    // INT0 rising edge (or falling)
  GICR  |= (1<<INT0);                // arm trigger
  // ISR(INT0_vect) → timestamp / begin sampling

  So the same comparator that does the SAR binary search doubles as the trigger 
  comparator — you just point DACV at a fixed level instead of sweeping it.
  That's a proper rising/falling-edge scope trigger for zero added parts.

  (You'd switch the firmware between two uses of PD2/INT0: poll it during a SAR
  conversion, or arm INT0 when you want edge-triggered capture — never both at
  once.)

  So your scope can do level-triggered capture later, just by reusing the
  comparator + INT0 you already have. Nice payoff from the pin choice.

  Want to keep going — fold the scope front-end into the docs, or start on
  firmware?

  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ right after each bnc connector                                                

● Yes — and I think I see your 4-relay plan now, and it's a good one for "strict
  control of what's in and out."

  Relay choice — both fine ✓

  - Songle 5VDC (SRD-05VDC-SL-C, 10A 250VAC/30VDC, Rs.100) ✓
  - JERQI 5VDC JQC-3F (T73) (10A) ✓ — equivalent, also fine

  Either works — 10A/30VDC contacts are massive overkill for your
  ±13V/low-current signal, which means very reliable, near-zero contact wear.
  Pick whichever's in stock. 5V coil (matches your logic), SPDT.

  PD2 stays for SAR — confirmed. ✓

  The 4 relays — here's a clean assignment

  ┌─────┬──────────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │  #  │    Relay     │       Where        │           Purpose            │
  ├─────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1   │ CH1 output   │ right after BNC1's │ hard-disconnect ch1 output   │
  │     │              │  protection        │ (enable / fault isolation)   │
  ├─────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2   │ CH2 output   │ right after BNC2's │ hard-disconnect ch2 output   │
  │     │              │  protection        │                              │
  ├─────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │     │              │ right after the    │ isolate the probe when not   │
  │ 3   │ Scope input  │ probe BNC          │ scoping (control what comes  │
  │     │              │ front-end          │ IN)                          │
  ├─────┼──────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │     │ 2ch mode     │ GainOffset →       │ open in 2ch mode so ch2      │
  │ 4   │ disconnect   │ summing-amp (R20)  │ doesn't mix into ch1         │
  │     │              │ path               │                              │
  └─────┴──────────────┴────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  That's your "3 at the BNC ports + 1 mode" = 4 relays.

  Why this is genuinely good (not over-engineering)

  - True galvanic disconnect: on fault (PD3), the MCU opens the output relays →
  the BNC is electrically removed from the circuit — far stronger than just
  muting the signal (muting leaves the op-amp wired to the BNC).
  - Strict I/O: each port is independently enable/disable-able → you control
  exactly what's connected.
  - All MCU-controlled (digital) → fits your no-manual-switch rule.
  - Relay contacts carry the signal with negligible resistance (~tens of mΩ) →
  no signal degradation. Switching is mechanical (~5ms) but that's fine —
  enable/disable/fault are infrequent, not per-sample.

  Driver (one per relay, all SL-common)

  GPIO ─[1k]─ base BC337 (or 2N2222)
              emitter → GND
              collector → relay coil → +5V
     flyback 1N4148 across coil (cathode→+5V)
  4 relays → 4 GPIOs (you have PC0–PC7 free, plenty) + 4 drivers.

  Fault behavior

  On PD3 overcurrent → MCU opens the output relays (CH1/CH2) → outputs truly
  isolated, plus mutes the gain bank. Belt-and-suspenders.

  Verdict

  Yes — 4 relays is a sound "strict control" design: CH1-out, CH2-out, scope-in,
  and the 2ch-mode disconnect. Songle/JERQI 5VDC are the right part. Each via a
  BC337+1N4148+1k driver on a spare PC pin.

  Want me to add the 4-relay control block to the docs (placement + driver + the
  fault-opens-output behavior)?

✻ Cooked for 4m 15s
